# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Security

- **Resolved a high-severity denial-of-service advisory in a transitive dependency (`brace-expansion`).** Bumped it to 5.0.8 in the CLI. The advisory is an unbounded expansion length that can exhaust memory and crash the process; the package arrives through `minimatch`, which is a runtime dependency, so the code path shipped. It reaches the expander from glob patterns in a project's own graph and coverage configuration rather than from anything an outside party controls, which bounds the practical exposure — but the fix costs nothing and the pattern source is not something the CLI should be relying on for safety.

### Added

- **A file can now satisfy coverage by matching an architecture type directly, with no node required.** Turn on the new `coverage.type_level: true` setting (graph schema 5.2.0; run `yg init --upgrade` on an existing project) and every uncovered file is classified against whichever types in `yg-architecture.yaml` declare a `when:` predicate. Matching exactly one such type quietly satisfies coverage under that type's own rules. Matching two or more is a new blocking error, `ambiguous-node-type`, naming every matching type and giving two ways to resolve it: declare an explicit node for the file with one of the matched types, or narrow one type's `when` so only one still matches — either way, existing verdicts elsewhere are unaffected. Both `ambiguous-node-type` and the file-unreadable case below block independent of the required/advisory tier split — an ambiguous or unreadable file is an architecture-level problem, not a coverage-area one, so it blocks even for a file outside every required root. (A file under an excluded root is the one exception: it is never classified at all, so neither error can fire there.) A file that also matches an `enforce: strict` type is left entirely to that type's own orphan/misplaced error rather than also being called ambiguous, and that error's message now names any other type the file matches too. A file whose matching type could not even be evaluated (for example a content-based rule against a file over the 5MB scan limit) is a blocking `file-unreadable` error instead of being silently skipped. None of the three above ever also shows up in the ordinary uncovered-file report — only a file matching no type at all still falls through there, and its message now says plainly that the architecture has no type for it, distinct from a file that matches a type but simply has no node. The suggested next command for an ambiguous file carries its own two-exit guidance rather than a generic, file-less pointer. `yg check`'s summary line names the split for files satisfied this way (`N/M files (X node-owned, Y type-covered, Z excluded)`), and prints a one-line notice whenever the setting is on but no type declares `when:` yet, so turning it on never quietly does nothing. A fresh `yg init` turns the setting on by default, with a comment explaining that it does nothing until a type gains a `when:` rule; existing projects keep it off until they opt in.

- **`coverage.type_level` now also gates the import edges reaching a classified file, not only which files count as classified.** Every statically-resolved import where AT LEAST ONE endpoint is a type-covered file is checked live, on every `yg check`, against the IMPORTING type's own `relations:` allow-list in `yg-architecture.yaml` — the same allow-list a declared node-to-node relation already answers to. (An import between two explicit nodes is unaffected — that stays `relation-undeclared-dependency`'s territory, same as before.) An import with no allowed relation type between the two types is a new blocking error, `type-relation-forbidden`, naming the pair and up to five sample violating files (with a remainder count beyond that), with the cheapest fix listed first: allow the pair in `yg-architecture.yaml`, promote the imported file to its own node with a curated relation, or remove the dependency. An import into a file matching two or more types is never gated at all — an ambiguous file isn't classified, so there is nothing to check it against — and resolving that ambiguity now says explicitly that either fix may surface fresh gate findings once the file's own imports join it. With the setting off, nothing changes: the gate has no classified files to look at.

- **A git-tracked file matched by `.gitignore` is now flagged wherever it appears, not only when a node mapping happens to cover it.** The coverage scan reads the filesystem directly, gitignore rules and all, so a git-tracked file a `.gitignore` rule excludes (for example one added with `git add -f`, or ignored after it was already tracked) was invisible to the previous, mapping-only detector — it ships in the repository, yet nothing checked it. The new `tracked-file-gitignored` check catches the anomaly regardless of mapping, reporting it as an error under a required coverage area and a warning elsewhere. A file under an excluded coverage area raises no issue at all — exclusion wins over the required tier here too, the same absolute rule every other coverage check now applies. A repository with no git history, or one where git can't be reached, is unaffected — the check simply doesn't run.

### Changed

- **`coverage.excluded` is now absolute, even against a more specific `coverage.required` root.** Previously the longer (more specific) of a matching required/excluded root pair won, so a required root nested inside a broader excluded root still blocked. Now any excluded-root match silences the file outright, independent of specificity — the same rule the type-level classification lattice already used internally, now unified across every coverage check (tiering, the tracked∩gitignored anomaly, and `yg init`'s agent-rules placement prediction). **This can LOOSEN an existing project at upgrade**: a file that used to be a blocking `unmapped-files` error under a nested required-inside-excluded config now silently passes. A new warning, `coverage-required-shadowed`, flags exactly this shape (a required root fully inside an excluded root) so the dead line is visible rather than silently inert.

- **`yg-config.yaml`'s `coverage:` section now rejects an unrecognized key instead of silently ignoring it.** A misspelled setting under `coverage:` no longer disappears without a trace — it now fails with a clear error naming the bad key. The section also gains the new recognized key described above, `coverage.type_level`, settable only in the committed config — a local, gitignored overlay can never change it.

- **Graph schema bumped 5.1.0 → 5.2.0.** A content-free bump — it exists only to gate `coverage.type_level` to projects that have upgraded, so an old CLI can never silently ignore a setting a newer one enforces. An existing project sees no behavior change from the bump alone; run `yg init --upgrade` when the CLI reports the version mismatch.

- **(Internal.) The "Also matches" hint's co-match filter on a `type-strict-orphan` error is now the same predicate the type-level classification lattice already used internally** (excludes every `enforce: strict` type, not just the one the error names), instead of a second, slightly different filter that happened to agree with it in every case reachable today — a file matching 2+ strict types is already fully intercepted by the separate strict-overlap-conflict check before this hint ever runs. No observable output changes; this is a consistency cleanup, not a bug fix.

- **`yg check --approve` and `--dry-run` now report exactly what garbage collection prunes.** Previously a pruned verdict — an aspect detached from a node, a node deleted, a rule that no longer matches — disappeared from the lock silently; the run just came back a little smaller with nothing said about why. Now, whenever anything was pruned, the run prints a summary line (a count, split into billed vs. free) followed by one line per pruned entry naming the aspect, the unit, and the reason. `--dry-run` shows the same summary as a preview of what a real `--approve` would prune, without writing anything. Nothing prints when nothing was pruned, and `--quiet` suppresses the summary on a real `--approve` fill exactly like the rest of the progress stream.

- **The deterministic-gate skip message now speaks in terms of the unit it skipped, not unconditionally "node."** When an enforced deterministic check's refusal skips a pair's paid LLM review, the explanation used to say a free check "rejects this node" no matter what was skipped. It now says "this unit" — wording that stays accurate whether the skipped review belongs to a whole component or, for a project that opts into per-file type coverage, a single file. Every adopter sees the updated phrasing; nothing else about the message changed.

### Removed

- **The `mapped-file-gitignored` error is retired**, superseded by the broader `tracked-file-gitignored` check described above. If a project suppressed `mapped-file-gitignored` with a `yg-suppress` marker, that marker is now inert — remove it; the code it named will simply never fire again.

### Fixed

- **A deterministic rule attached to a file covered only by its architecture type (no node of its own) no longer infra-errors on every run.** Under `coverage.type_level`, a rule attached to a type-covered file used to fail before its `check.mjs` ever executed, on every run, with no way to ever produce a real verdict — only LLM-reviewed rules on the same files actually ran. The rule now runs with an allowance derived from the architecture's own relation rules for the file's matched type (the same authority the live type-relation gate already uses): the file's own content, plus every file whose TRUE OWNER the architecture permits this type to depend on, whether that owner is a declared component or the file is itself enforced by its type alone. Ownership is resolved with the same child-wins authority the live type gate itself uses, so a permitted component's directory mapping can no longer leak a nested, forbidden component's file into the allowance just because that file sits textually inside the directory. `ctx.node` and `ctx.graph` are unavailable on such a run — there is no component to back either — and touching them fails the same way an undeclared read does, naming the two ways out (keep the rule to files the architecture already permits this type to reach, or give the file a component of its own) in the actual printed diagnostic, not only on the underlying error. A cached refusal on one covered file can no longer suppress paid review of a different covered file matching the same type.

- **A deterministic check's runtime-error diagnostic now names the actual next step for whatever broke, instead of always saying "fix check.mjs."** When `check.mjs` fails to run for any reason — the ctx.node/ctx.graph-unavailable case above, a node path that no longer resolves in the graph, an async return, a malformed `Violation`, and every other structural fault the runner can raise — the printed notice used to replace the specific guidance with one generic instruction regardless of what actually went wrong, so an agent could see WHAT broke but never HOW to fix it. It now surfaces the real next step every time: for example, a missing node now says to pass an existing node path or add the node to the graph, instead of blaming a check.mjs bug that was never the problem.

- **The quality gate no longer refuses a correct commit at random.** One test in the unit tier made a live call to a model provider with a two-minute budget; whenever that provider was slow the test timed out, the whole test step failed, and a commit that was perfectly fine was rejected — passing on an immediate retry. It was also self-inflicting: the more verification a session did through that same provider, the likelier the next gate run failed for reasons unrelated to the change. The repository already had a tier for tests that need an external service, excluded from the default run and from CI, with two live-provider tests already in it; this one was simply in the wrong place. Nothing was lost by moving it — the response shape it asserted is proven without a network by the parse-level tests, twenty-four times over — and the unit tier went from a two-minute budget to under a second.

- **The repository's own analysis instruments no longer discard the end of their own reports.** Five of them ended by calling for an immediate exit, which on a pipe abandons output still queued for writing — so anything past roughly 26 KB vanished while the run still reported success. The part lost is the tail, and the tail is where each one prints its mandatory caveats: how small the sample was, that an unmeasurable value is not a zero, and which event streams were actually read. A truncated report was indistinguishable from a clean one that simply had nothing to qualify, which is the most dangerous shape this kind of output can take. Reaching the end of a script already exits successfully, so the call was removed.

- **A suppression marker closed with the HTML spec's `--!>` form no longer keeps the terminator as its justification.** Only `-->` was stripped from a marker's reason text. In a Markdown file closed the other legal way, the terminator survived into the recorded reason — and a marker written with no justification at all ended up with `--!>` as its reason, which is non-empty, so it passed the check that exists to make every waiver carry one. Both closers are now recognised, restoring that rejection.

- **`yg type-suggest` no longer reports a plain "no match" for a type whose classification rule couldn't actually be checked.** A file over the 5MB content-scan limit, evaluated against a type whose rule inspects file content, used to come back as an ordinary non-match — indistinguishable from a file that genuinely fails every rule. The command now lists such types separately, naming the reason the rule couldn't be applied, so it's clear the rule was never actually evaluated rather than evaluated and failed.

## [5.6.0] - 2026-07-25

### Documentation

- **The command reference covers every command the CLI registers.** `yg portal` was absent from the page entirely, and `yg advise` and `yg incident` were described but listed in no summary table. `yg prime` is now a subsection of Setup in the same form as every other entry rather than a top-level heading appended after it, `yg init` has an explicit heading, and the parenthesised counts in the section titles match what is listed under them.
- **Issue codes the CLI emits are documented.** Added to the reference: `structural-cycle`, `aspect-review-by-malformed`, `aspect-review-overdue`, `rules-digest-stale`. Added on the pages where they belong: the four `enforce: strict` errors, the four port-contract errors (only `port-missing-consumes` was described before), `event-unpaired`, `relation-parse-failed`, the three `when-unknown-*` reference-integrity errors, `description-missing`, `overlapping-mapping`, and the two gitignore false-green errors.
- **`enforce: strict` and the log gate now have adopter documentation.** Neither had any. `enforce: strict` is described where node types are defined — the type-shopping gap it closes, when not to use it, the impact preview to run first, and each of its errors. The log gate's mechanics are on the lock page: when an entry is required (including that it does not depend on the component having any rules), what positive closure records, that one missing entry stops a whole approve run from filling anything, the append-only correction convention, and the merge procedure.
- **The two optional rule fields `review_by:` and `errs:` are documented.** Both are load-bearing — `errs:` drives the waiver inventory's warning about waiving a check that cannot false-positive, and `review_by:` produces a standing non-blocking warning once its day passes — and neither was defined outside the embedded schema reference.
- **Corrections where the documentation contradicted the implementation.** `ctx.files` is the unit's subject view, not an alias for the component's full file set — they differ whenever a `per: file` unit or a `scope.files` filter narrows the subject. The reviewers guide also gained `ctx.subject` and `file.language`, corrected its violation shape, and its copyable example now guards `file.ast` before use. The claim that per-language file filtering was "designed but not yet built" is gone: there is no `language:` field and no plan for one. The aspect schema no longer reads as if `per:` had a default inside a `scope:` block — writing `scope:` without `per:` is an error. `.symbols-cache/` is labelled as the retired predecessor of `.ast-cache/`. The platforms page accounts for every legacy artifact the installer removes, not two of six.
- **Documented behaviours that were only discoverable by hitting them.** A typo'd top-level key in `yg-config.yaml` is silently ignored while a typo under `reviewer:`, inside a tier, or under `signals:`/`events:` is a hard error. The full list of local, never-committed state under `.yggdrasil/` is on the configuration page (three entries appeared nowhere before). The suppress surface gained multi-rule markers, the whole-file waiver and where it has to sit, and the fourth warning the inventory emits. The attention feed's eleven nomination classes are listed in their real priority order.
- **New section: how to tell whether a rule is any good.** The live diagnostic, a drill corpus and what its five outcomes mean, replaying a script rule over history, and measuring a judge's self-consistency existed only in the embedded knowledge base and in per-command reference entries, with no page tying them together for someone authoring a rule.
- **Two surfaces that need something the package does not contain now say so.** The model-swap protocol presented `scripts/mcnemar.mjs` and `scripts/metamorphic.mjs` as runnable, but the published package ships only the built CLI. Both are now linked in the repository with their real status: the McNemar analysis is a dependency-free single file that runs anywhere, to copy and adapt; the metamorphic probe is written against this repository's own build output and fixture paths and will not run elsewhere unchanged, so it is presented as a worked reference for the technique rather than a tool. The attention feed's candidate-rule-family class reads a suggestions file produced by an offline pass that is likewise not in the package, so that class stays silent unless you supply the file — present-or-omit by design, and its absence is not an error.
- **The embedded knowledge base gained four things only the adopter docs knew.** Agents read the knowledge base rather than `docs/`, so a rule an agent can hit blind belongs there: structural relations must form a DAG (declaring the reverse direction to satisfy the undeclared-dependency check just trades one blocking error for another); file classification auto-exempts every path under the graph directory, which means a type's `when` gives no filtering when modelling the graph's own rule files; the CLI providers' built-in model fallbacks, distinguished from `yg init --model`'s own default; and that YAML and TOML have parse trees, not only JSON.
- **The shipped operating manual no longer sends an agent into a dead end.** Its guidance for clearing an undeclared-dependency refusal named two exits without saying that the structural relation types must stay acyclic, so on a mutual dependency the first exit produces a second blocking error and the real resolution appeared nowhere. It also gained three mechanisms an agent could otherwise only meet as an uninterpretable error — the gitignore false-green, the waiver-inventory warning about un-waivable checks, and the reviewer-tier dry-fit — plus the two optional rule labels, and its pointer to the fuller reference no longer lists commands already in its own table.
- **Every quoted transcript in the example projects matches what the commands print.** All seven result headers understated their file count, having been written before the universal agent-rules install added four tracked files to each example, and none carried the verified-pair breakdown the header has since gained. The examples index claimed every example ships a README when the two reviewer examples never had one, and a keyless config comment asserted that the schema requires a reviewer tier — a script-only project may omit the whole section.
- **This repository's own contributor and agent instructions were corrected.** The agent instructions described the quality gate as seven categories that "cover everything"; it is sixteen steps, including a portal end-to-end suite that drives Chromium through Playwright and fails outright when Chromium is absent, and a digest-freshness step that checks both installed artifacts at the repo root *and* in every example carrying its own graph. The contributing guide's rules-refresh recipe named a menu option that no longer exists and omitted the required rebuild, its architecture pointer sent readers to a README with no architecture overview, and the `scripts/` directory — eleven measurement instruments, seven of them referenced nowhere — had no index or statement of why none of them is a CLI command.
- **The shipped agent operating manual now warns against calling a failure "pre-existing" without evidence that actually establishes it.** Two real misdiagnoses in this repository reached that conclusion by methods that could not support it — isolating a change by stashing only its own uncommitted edits (which cannot distinguish "unrelated to this change" from "already broken before this branch"), and treating a single clean re-run as proof (which a freshly introduced flake produces just as reliably). The rule shipped to every adopting repository via `yg prime` now states plainly that a single passing re-run is not sufficient evidence. This guidance sits between the workflow steps for after modifying code and for the end of a conversation, in the same chronological order as the rest of that list, rather than after the end-of-conversation step where it previously read out of sequence.
- **The `utility` component type's description now states the constraint that sent two pieces of shared logic there.** Two modules ended up under `utility` specifically because it is the only type reachable from both the template layer and the engine — the sole legal home for logic those two layers must share, a consequence of the allowed relations rather than a preference — but that reasoning previously lived only in the modules' own file headers and one node description, so it had to be rediscovered (once from an explicitly wrong instruction) before the graph refused the alternative. The architecture file's own type description now states it directly, where anyone choosing a location would look first.
- **The getting-started and reviewer walkthroughs now show the CLI's real output, and more embedded guidance was resynced with actual behavior.** The sample `yg check` / `yg check --approve` transcripts in the getting-started and reviewers guides were rewritten to the current grouped format — a single verdict-and-metrics header line, `Errors (N)` blocks with a shared why + fix, and the real refusal shape — replacing a pre-redesign layout (verbose multi-line header, per-pair `SATISFIED`/`REFUSED` lines, and a `Result: PASS/FAIL` footer) that the CLI has not printed for some time. The README's CI recipe now rebuilds the free deterministic-verdict cache before the gate (a fresh checkout has none, since that cache is gitignored) and no longer describes the lock as a single committed file — it is a committed/gitignored split. The suppress-syntax topic now documents the trailing-same-line-comment footgun honestly (in a grammar file a marker written after code on the same line IS honored — it waives the line below it — so a same-line marker must never be relied on to waive the code it sits on); the strict-classification list gained the `enforce-strict-without-when` code; and the LLM-aspect topic's prompt-too-large remedy no longer claims that raising a tier's limit cascades re-verification (tier config, including `max_prompt_chars`, is excluded from the pair hash). The node schema reference now documents the glob mapping form, the child-precedence ownership carve-out, and the `event_name` field that pairs `emits`/`listens`. The dogfood showcase's stale hardcoded counts were refreshed against the live graph and its relation table now records the one real `extends` relation instead of listing it as deferred.
- **The CLI reference gained the sections it was missing and corrected a couple of counts.** The user-facing command reference now documents `yg drill` (replay a rule over its case corpus) and `yg schemas` (the field reference for each graph element) — both shipped but previously undocumented there. The config-schema reference now documents the `events:` section (opting into a committed, team-shared LLM-fill event stream), and the `yg impact --file` reason list now names all five reasons a node can be affected (the cold-start "may observe this file" case was missing).
- **The embedded guidance and the agent rules were corrected where they had drifted from how the tool actually behaves.** The most consequential: the mandatory-log gate is now described as **all-or-nothing** at approve time — if any component that requires a justification entry changed without one, the approve step verifies nothing that run (it does not skip only that component while others proceed) — in both the log-management topic and the agent rules that ship to every adopter. The deterministic-check authoring topic now names the runtime error codes the fill path actually raises (the `STRUCTURE_CHECK_*` family, with the ad-hoc/drill path's `AST_CHECK_*` noted), corrects "runs in the main process" to the worker-thread pool it actually uses, lists the drill run as a third place a check executes, and stops calling a JSON file "non-parseable" (JSON has a grammar). The reviewer-tier note now states plainly that only a tier's NAME is part of a verdict's identity — editing a named tier's provider/model/config re-verifies nothing. The per-call `timeout` is documented as honored by the local `ollama` provider too, not "CLI providers only," in the configuration topic, the config schema, and the configuration guide. The strict-classification error list now includes the overlap-conflict code. And the agent rules' seven-channel example was made domain-neutral (no CLI-repo-specific rule names), with the shipped `drill` / `simulate` / `structure` / `incident` / `portal` commands added so an adopter's agent can discover them.

### Added

- **`yg prime` prints the full agent operating manual straight from the installed CLI; `yg prime --digest` prints the small canonical block that belongs committed in a repository.** The manual is no longer written into a project by `yg init` as a standing file — an agent instead runs `yg prime` at the start of a session, and any time it wants to re-read the current rules, and always gets exactly what the installed CLI ships, never a stale copy left over from an earlier version. `--digest` prints only the short standing summary: the mandate to run `yg prime` before making any change, plus the handful of invariants no reviewer can enforce (never write a suppression without the user's approval, never change a rule's review date, treat `yg advise` items and incidents as proposals, never hand-edit the lock) — the exact content `yg init` commits inside the digest block.
- **Starting a project without a reviewer is a choice you can now make in a terminal, either by answering the setup question or by asking for it up front.** The setup wizard's provider list ends with **"None for now"**, and `yg init --no-reviewer` does the same thing without asking anything — in a terminal or out of one. Both are first-class: a project with no reviewer still runs its script rules, its dependency control and the whole `yg check` gate, for free and with no key, and a reviewer can be added the day a judgment rule needs one. Previously the only way to get there in a terminal was to run the command with its input detached, which was documented nowhere. Choosing "None for now" from the reconfiguration menu of a project that already has a reviewer leaves that reviewer exactly as it was, and `--no-reviewer` is refused with an explanation when it is combined with reviewer flags or `--upgrade`, or aimed at a project that already has a graph — it decides how to start a new project, and never removes a reviewer an existing one configured.
- **`yg check` gained a warning, `rules-digest-stale`, that catches a committed agent-rules digest that has drifted from the installed CLI.** It hashes the digest block inside `AGENTS.md`'s markers and the `.clinerules/yggdrasil.md` file against the installed CLI's own canonical digest hash, checks that the `@AGENTS.md` import line is present in `CLAUDE.md` (that line is presence-checked, not hashed — there is nothing in it to hash), and reports whichever of missing / hand-edited / from-an-older-CLI / duplicated applies, always pointing at `yg init --upgrade`. It is a warning, never a blocking error, and it writes nothing — like the built-in relation-conformance check, it is recomputed live on every run, never cached. Before this check existed, a stale or hand-edited digest was silently invisible: nothing told you an agent might be reading outdated rules.

- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** File-ownership resolution is now single-source, and a new enforced deterministic rule keeps it that way. "Which component owns this file?" had been re-implemented in four places that drifted apart — one of them (behind `yg owner` / `yg context --file` / `yg impact --file`) used a length-first rule that disagreed with the hierarchy-first rule the check verifies against (see the Fixed entry). The two duplicate resolvers were folded into the one canonical resolver, which now also exposes the matched-mapping detail the command surface needs, and the length-first one was corrected to defer to it. The new rule — *single-source owner resolution* — forbids importing the child-precedence comparator anywhere except the one canonical module, so the rule cannot be re-implemented and drift again; it is AST-based, alias-proof, and zero-false-positive by design, bundled into the source-hygiene rule set alongside the existing "no direct minimatch import" boundary it mirrors.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** A new enforced deterministic rule — *prototype-safe registry lookup* — guards against a bug class this sweep hit three times: a value read from a module-level plain-object lookup table by a runtime key, without an own-property guard, so a key that collides with a name inherited from the base object prototype (`constructor`, `toString`, `hasOwnProperty`, `valueOf`, `__proto__`) reads an inherited value instead of "not found" and either crashes or fabricates output. The rule is file-local and zero-false-positive by design — it flags only the clean, provable same-file shape (a `Record<string, V>` const read by a bare identifier key, unguarded, in a read position) and stays silent on everything it cannot prove from the file's own syntax tree; it is bundled into the existing source-hygiene rule set so it applies to all first-party source. Turning it on immediately surfaced four more internal lookup tables (language display names, grammar/WASM resolution, the feature-vector vocabulary, and the check-summary label glossary) plus one test helper that had the same latent gap; all were hardened with an `Object.hasOwn` guard (behavior for every real key unchanged, a reserved name now correctly reads as absent).
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** Every one of the CLI's commands is now modeled as a flow, so the what/why/next message contract is enforced uniformly across the entire command surface. The final group — the decision log, entry-point search, the flow/waiver/type-suggestion inventories, the reference browsers (knowledge and schemas), and on-demand aspect testing — joined either their matching read-only query flow (navigation or analysis) or a new small flow of their own (decision log, reference browsing, aspect diagnostics), following the two report-output and arg-parser exemptions so their listings and reference content are treated as results while their genuine errors (unknown topic, unknown schema, bad node path, merge-conflict) stay structured.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** The six read-only attention/diagnostics CLI commands were modeled as flows, completing the convention that a command's process is a graph flow carrying the what/why/next message contract: incident recording, drill replay, rule simulation, the structure dashboard, the portal view, and the advisory attention feed. This enforces — for the first time — that each command's agent-facing diagnostics derive from the shared structured-message builder, across the command node and its participants. The design was pressure-tested by an adversarial multi-lens panel; its correctness guards were applied (drill omits the determinism contract because its LLM-drill path bills the reviewer; the read-only commands carry it where they make no reviewer calls; portal carries the POSIX-path-output contract). To model the read-only report commands cleanly, the shared what/why/next rule was clarified with two principled exemptions — read-only **report/attention/findings output** (a dashboard, an attention item, a per-case replay outcome) is the answer the agent asked for, not a failure diagnostic, and a framework-level **Commander argument-parser validation error** is the arg-parsing layer, not the command's own diagnostic surface — while the command's genuine error paths (bad graph, infra failure, blocking refusal) still must be structured. Enforcing the new flows surfaced and fixed a real bug (the portal static-write confirmation printed an OS-native path to stdout without POSIX normalization) and two deferred dogfood items (structure/runner.ts and drill-runner.ts each emit a few remediation-bearing lines from an engine module as flat strings rather than structured messages — a focused follow-up).
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** A new enforced, zero-false-positive rule — `docs-internal-links` — now guards this repository's own adopter documentation against broken internal links: any inline link on a doc page whose target does not resolve to an existing doc page is refused, so a link that would 404 for a reader cannot ship. It reads the whole doc set at once (the adopter guides were consolidated into a single graph node so the resolver can see every page — a split would make a link to a page in a sibling node read as broken), and it under-approximates by design (`errs: under`): it resolves only what it can with certainty and skips external links, assets (any non-`.md` extension), same-page anchors, image links, reference-style links, and anything inside a fenced or indented code block or an inline code span, so every violation it reports is a provably dead link. Its zero-false-positive claim was hardened against a multi-lens adversarial review (titled links, angle-bracket destinations, weird-extension and extensionless images, `.html`/`.gif`/uppercase assets, escaped brackets, multi-backtick code spans, mismatched-marker and indented code fences, balanced-paren and percent-encoded targets, root and index-page aliases) and verified over the real docs. Because the drill corpus excludes `.md` files and runs one file per case, a multi-file link resolver cannot be drilled; its regression fixture is a dedicated end-to-end test over real `.md` files through the public CLI surface, and it carries a reasoned entry in the drill-exemption list.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** The example-must-be-self-contained rule graduated from advisory to enforced — it holds a clean record, ships a falsifiable drill corpus, and its detection under-approximates (`errs: under`), so a refusal is always a real violation and enforcing it can never block correct code. The three other rules considered for promotion were deliberately kept advisory: each over-approximates (`errs: over`), so it is a soft drift signal that could refuse compliant code if enforced — exactly the class the repository keeps as a live advisory exemplar rather than a blocking law. Separately, the `repo-config` classifier was narrowed to stop overlapping `ci-config`: workflow files under `.github/workflows/**` and `.markdownlint*` now classify only as CI config, so a workflow or lint file misfiled into a repo-config node is caught rather than silently accepted.
- **The architecture file is now checked for the kinds of silent typos that used to weaken enforcement without warning.** Three gaps closed. A misspelled key on a component-type definition — `parent` for `parents`, `aspect` for `aspects`, `relation` for `relations` — was silently ignored, quietly dropping whatever hierarchy or rule attachment the author intended; it is now a blocking error naming the bad key and the valid ones. A relation allow-list that names a target component type which does not exist (a typo of a real type) is now caught up front — before, a component of the intended type simply never matched the misspelled entry, so the relation was over-restricted with no signal; the check is the relation-side counterpart of the existing undefined-parent check. And a review tier declared on a rule that has no language-model reviewer of its own — an aggregate bundle — is now rejected the same way it already was for script-based rules, since a tier only ever names a language-model configuration. (While hardening these paths, two pre-existing spots that could emit an un-normalized file path into a diagnostic were also fixed.)
- **`yg check` now warns when a rule is attached to a component type as a default but its own applicability filter switches it off on every component of that type.** A rule can be listed as a default for a type in the architecture, yet carry a `when` condition that excludes every component of that type — so it reads as enforced for the type but silently verifies nothing there. The check reports this as a non-blocking warning naming the rule and the type, reusing the same applicability engine the verifier uses (so it can never disagree with what is actually enforced); it stays silent for a type that has no components yet, and never fails the build. This catches the class of mistake where a rule's `when` cancels the architecture's own attachment of it to a sibling type.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** "No drill, no enforced" is now a test: every enforced deterministic rule in this repository must ship a `violates-*` drill fixture (a falsifiable regression case proving the rule refuses what it claims to), or appear in an explicit, reasoned exemption list — the rules whose check needs graph or exact-file-path context the single-file drill model cannot provide. The exemption list cannot grow silently or go stale.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** Three more of this repository's own rules were sharpened to catch what they claim. The portal "the one write must shell the CLI" rule was structurally blind to the shipped code: it only inspected a spawn call's own argument array, but the real code builds the `['check','--approve', …]` vector in a helper and spreads it into the spawn, so the rule verified nothing. It now checks every CLI argument vector (any array carrying the `check` token must also carry a literal `--approve`) and bans reading process-environment values anywhere in a spawn's arguments — with drill fixtures in the real helper-and-spread shape, including the exact regression the old rule missed. The WASM-tree-lifecycle rule, which forbids importing the raw parse function that leaks a native tree, now also catches reaching it through a namespace import (`import * as p … p.parseFile(…)`), not only a named import. And the cascade rule guarding raw prompt/response from being logged before redaction was moved onto the shared LLM node that actually assembles prompts and verifies responses, off the registry barrel that carries nothing to protect.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** The rule requiring command errors to be built through the shared message builder is no longer silently switched off on the command-support layer. Its own applicability filter pinned it to the `command` type, cancelling the architecture's attachment of it to `command-support` — the exact layer that holds the canonical error helpers — so that code was unguarded. The filter is removed (the architecture attachment and the file glob already scope it correctly), and its detection was upgraded from a fixed character-window heuristic to an AST-scoped one that recognises a message built via the shared builder anywhere in the enclosing function, which removes a false alarm on compliant multi-branch error handling.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** Three of this repository's own rules were brought back into sync with the code they guard. The single-source graph-query rule's reserved-name list was regenerated from the actual `core/graph/` export surface — it had drifted to seven names against ~two dozen exports, leaving the aspect-resolution helpers it exists to protect unguarded, and still listed one deleted helper; the language-registry names are deliberately left out as too generic to reserve safely. The two "route this through the shared helper" rules (`no-direct-fs`, `no-direct-minimatch`) now skip a compile-time-only `import type` — which creates no runtime dependency — restoring their no-false-positives guarantee, with drill cases pinning the type-only shape. And the LLM provider-retry rule's description (and its drill fixture) no longer require a `getContextWindowSize()` method that was removed from the provider interface weeks ago.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** A new enforced, zero-false-positive rule — `no-shell-injection` — now guards this repository's own source against shell-command injection: it refuses a shell command built by template substitution or string concatenation and passed to `exec`/`execSync`, and any `child_process` call that re-enables the shell with `shell: true`. It is binding-aware (only a call whose target resolves to a `child_process` exec/spawn binding can trip it, so `RegExp.prototype.exec` and a static-literal `exec` are never flagged), so it fires only on a provable injection surface (`errs: under`). It rides the existing `source-hygiene` baseline bundle, so it applies to every first-party source node in one attach point, and ships with a drill corpus pinning the violating and compliant shapes.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** A new enforced deterministic rule — `runcheck-injected-input-parity` — closes a gap where the same repository reported a different `yg check` issue set depending on which surface called the check engine. The engine keeps no clock and reads no files itself; some of its checks are gated on an optional caller-supplied input, and an omitted input is silently skipped rather than erroring — this has already caused two real defects (a warning vanishing under `--approve`, then again in the portal), each caught only by manual review. The rule reads the engine's own source to derive which inputs currently gate an issue (rather than hardcoding the list, so a future fifth gated input is picked up automatically) and confirms every call site supplies all of them. It also requires every optional field of the engine's options type to be *classified* — either derived as issue-gating from the engine's own body, or named in the rule's short side-effect allowlist with a reason — because a new gate written in any other shape would otherwise never be derived, never be demanded at a call site, and never be noticed; an unclassified field is refused by name until someone decides which it is. Everything but the component id and that allowlist is derived from a single parse of the engine's source (the function, its options parameter's name and position, the options type and its members, and the gating key set), so a parameter reorder can never move the positional half without the key half. It stays zero-false-positive by design (`errs: under`): a call whose options argument cannot be read statically — a variable, a spread, a computed key, an unrecognized key shape — is skipped rather than reported, and an inline comment among the arguments no longer shifts the read. It is attached individually to the components that actually call the engine rather than broadly by relation (the test suite deliberately calls the engine with partial inputs to exercise the skip behavior itself), and that attachment is pinned by a test that compares it against every real call site in the repository. Because the rule must read another component's source to derive its own check, it cannot run in the single-file drill sandbox and carries a reasoned exemption there; its regression coverage is instead two permanent fixture projects — one exercising every call shape it must refuse and every shape it must leave alone, one drifting a gated input out from under the derivation — driven through the real built binary with the real rule copied in per run.
- **A new offline "family without a law" miner proposes rules the codebase seems to want but does not yet have — as reviewable suggestions only, never an automatic rule.** `scripts/family-without-law.mjs` reads the per-file structural shape the parser already records, and within each language (never across languages) it looks for a tight group of near-identical files that share no narrow rule of their own — the fingerprint of a convention that ought to be written down but is not. For each such group it drafts a ready-to-refine scope (a path pattern, or a content pattern cut from the shared structure) so a proposal always comes with its reach evidence; a group it cannot cut a faithful pattern for is dropped rather than guessed. It is fully offline and read-only, makes no AI calls, never affects whether a build passes, and — the hard guarantee — never creates or writes a rule; its one output is a local, ignored suggestions file for a person to weigh. "No shared rule" is deliberately narrow: broad, inherited, and type-default rules are excluded, so a group only surfaces when nothing specific already governs it. Admission is gated on proven precision at build time — planted-family fixtures (single-language and mixed TypeScript/Python) pin exact recall, zero false groups, correct per-language tagging, no cross-language merge, and identical results across repeated runs.

- **`yg advise` now also proposes candidate rule families discovered by structural clustering, and suggests architectural cuts where module groups depend on each other in a loop — both as data with evidence, both requiring your sign-off, never applied automatically.** The attention feed gains two more suggestion classes, ranked below every existing one. The first surfaces the offline miner's finding — a tight group of near-identical files that share no rule of their own — naming the member files, the fitted scope pattern, and its tightness as quoted evidence, and proposing you draft a rule for exactly that scope (the rationale is always yours to supply, never invented); it appears only while the miner's suggestions file is fresh for the current structural-analysis format, so a moved format omits it rather than showing a stale group. The second reads the committed graph's declared dependencies and points out two or more module groups that depend on each other in a loop, naming the groups plainly and proposing either a cut or a contract across the boundary; because it comes straight from the committed graph it reads the same on every machine, and a loop is reported once, at the coarsest level it appears. Both share the same ten-item cap as the other suggestions — they compete for those slots, they do not add their own — and neither ever changes a verification result, blocks a build, or appears as a required next step.

- **The rule-health view now shows how often each rule's refusals were later waived or overturned — an honest signal of a rule that may block wrongly.** `yg aspects --health` gains a **false-block** (`fp`) column: for each rule, how many of its past refusals a person later waived (a `yg-suppress` marker now covers the refused code) or overturned (the block was re-approved after a waiver moved, rather than a genuine code fix — a real fix, with no waiver, never counts). It is a plain count with a small-sample honesty label ("not much to go on yet"), never a bare rate, and the two reviewer kinds are kept separate because they produce false alarms by different mechanisms. It feeds a human retirement ritual — think of a repo-level *false-block budget* each rule spends by its rate, so retiring the worst offenders makes room for new rules — and, like every signal in this view, it never blocks a build, changes a verdict, or shows up as a required action. The plain `yg aspects` listing is unchanged.
- **Teams can opt into a committed, shared record of LLM verification events — one config key.** Set `events: { committed_llm: true }` in `yg-config.yaml` and, from then on, each time the reviewer verifies a rule the event is recorded in a committed file (`.yggdrasil/yg-events.llm.jsonl`) shared with the whole team instead of a local-only file — so everyone sees the same history of what was reviewed and when. The shared file is union-merged across branches (events from different branches combine on merge instead of conflicting), and any sensitive rationale is stripped from the shared copy so a refusal note can never leak code fragments into your history. Deterministic checks never write to it, so a keyless CI run adds nothing and produces no churn. A machine on an older version keeps recording locally and never breaks the shared file; anything that reads these events combines the two and says plainly that older machines do not contribute, so the shared record is never mistaken for the whole picture. The opt-in is off by default, and turning it on or off never changes any verification result — it re-verifies nothing.
- **A repository incident ledger — `yg incident add` records what escaped enforcement and how, tagged by cause, as the tower's only external reality check.** Everything else the tool tells you is the graph reasoning about itself; an incident is a human writing down a real miss — a concern that shipped with no rule, a rule that fired on the wrong thing, a reviewer that was blind to what mattered, a lone judge that missed what a panel would have caught, or a gap that was not really about enforcement at all. Each entry is one timestamped, human-signed note appended to a committed file (`.yggdrasil/incidents.md`) that lives in your history and is reviewed in diffs, but is never treated as code and never reviewed by any rule. Recording one is your decision, the same way a waiver is: the tool never invents an incident, and it rejects an unrecognized cause with the list of valid ones and writes nothing. The ledger is append-only and its entries are kept in time order; because it is testimony that must never be able to break your build, `yg check` only **warns** (never blocks) if the dates fall out of order — the sign of a hand-edit or a messy merge — and stays silent when there is no ledger at all. `yg advise` surfaces the running count as a single reality-check line (shown even at zero, so you never forget the outside reference exists), and when any incident is marked as a miscalibrated rule it adds a plain note that your rules themselves may need a second look. The command only ever appends to the ledger — it never changes a verification result or whether your build passes.
- **You can now point a recorded miss at the specific rule that let it through, and see that attribution per-rule in the rule-health view.** `yg incident add` gains an optional `--aspect <id>`: when you record a miss caused by a miscalibrated rule (`--tag wrong-rule`), you can name the rule it slipped past. The id must be one of your existing rules — an unknown id is turned away with the same guidance as an unknown cause, and nothing is written. `yg aspects --health` then shows a **wrong-rule** column: for each rule, how many recorded misses were attributed to it, shown as an honest count with a plain "not much to go on yet" label because incident notes are sparse, hand-written testimony rather than a measured rate. The honesty boundary is explicit: a `wrong-rule` miss recorded *without* naming a rule still counts in the repository-wide reality-counter that `yg advise` shows, but it never surfaces against any one rule. Attribution is optional and backward-compatible — every previously recorded incident still reads exactly as before — and, like everything in this view, it never changes a verification result, never blocks a build, and never appears as a required action.
- **`yg simulate` replays a candidate checkable rule across the history it can honestly reach, so you can see what it would have caught before you commit to enforcing it.** Point it at an existing checkable rule and a component, and it replays that rule over your recent commits — each in a throwaway copy of the project, one commit at a time, strictly read-only — and reports, per commit, one of three plainly-labelled outcomes: the rule ran and found nothing, the rule refused some files (with a count), or the commit could not be honestly compared. A commit is "not comparable" when it pre-dates the project's setup (it has no rules of its own) or when its saved project format differs from today's and would need an upgrade this replay never performs — reported as such, never quietly counted as a clean pass. Your working files are never touched: everything happens in an isolated copy, and a safety guard makes sure a commit from before setup can never fall back to reading today's rules by mistake. Only checkable rules can be replayed — a rule that relies on a language-model's judgment is turned away, because such a verdict is a one-time reading, not something a rerun can reproduce; test those with `yg drill` instead. It is a report, not a gate: it always finishes successfully whatever it finds, never changes whether your build passes, and prints a plain caveat that past history was already filtered by the old rule gate, so the counts are bounds rather than the whole truth.
- **The portal has a new Dependency structure view — the shape of how your components depend on one another, in plain language.** Opened from the side, it shows three things: the dependencies that reach farthest across the component tree (named in words — how many levels each one spans, and whether a declared contract backs it), how the component groups at each level of the tree depend on one another (and what share of those cross-group dependencies form a loop, where groups depend on each other in a circle), and how far a change tends to travel — the share of the system an average component can reach through its dependencies. It is honest about its own limits: if the dependency scan cannot run it says the structure is unknown rather than drawing an empty graph, and on a project with too few components it shows the raw reach figure without over-reading it as a meaningful average. Event-style relations are deliberately left out of this picture and the view says so. It is read-only and recomputed live from the committed graph — the same picture `yg structure` prints on the command line — and never affects whether the build passes.
- **`yg advise` now points out "hot spots" — components that change often but have no rule covering them — so the code most in motion does not stay the least protected.** The attention feed gains one more kind of suggestion: a component whose files have changed across many of the recent commits, yet which carries no enforced rule, is nominated for coverage. Each hot spot shows how many of the last commits touched it, a short sample of the changed files, and the commit window those numbers came from — quoted as plain evidence, never as an instruction — and asks you to consider adding a rule or bringing the component under coverage, which is always your decision, never an automatic rule. A hot spot clears itself the moment a rule or coverage lands there or its recent activity ages out of the window, so the list stays current on its own. The activity count is read only from your git history: on a shallow clone or a checkout with no git history the suggestion is simply omitted rather than guessed at, and it never blocks anything, never changes a verification result, and never appears in the suggested next step. It ranks below every structural signal, so it never crowds out a more urgent finding.
- **`yg context --file` may now show a single advisory note when a file looks structurally unusual among its node's other same-language files — a hint to read more carefully, never a rule.** When you ask for a file's context and that file sits far from its same-language siblings on the structural measures a plain check already records, the command adds one plain-language line suggesting a closer read; no dimension names, scores, or jargon appear in it. It never blocks — the command still succeeds — and it appears only when the local structural index still describes the file's exact current contents, so a file edited since the last check stays silent. It is on by default; turn it off with `signals: { attention: false }` in `yg-config.yaml`. The flagging is calibrated to stay rare rather than chatty — the default sensitivity is tuned so only the clearest structural outliers surface, and an adopter can recalibrate it for their own codebase — and nothing about it ever changes, or is changed by, whether your code passes.
- **`yg check` now keeps a local, gitignored index of files that look structurally unusual among their node's other same-language files — pure attention, never a gate.** Building on the per-file structural fingerprint already recorded during parsing, a plain check now maintains a small local file listing only those source files that sit far from their neighbours on some structural dimension (size, nesting, or the counts of functions/classes/imports/branches/calls/literals), compared strictly within one node and one language and only when there are enough peer files to compare against — too few, and it stays silent. It is computed for free from the warm parse cache, is written best-effort (a failure to write it never fails a check), and is never an error, an exit code, or a suggested next step: it exists only so a later feature can point attention at files worth a closer read. A hidden `--attention-dump` prints the raw measurements grouped by node and language, marks the outliers, and exits without writing anything — a calibration aid for tuning how sensitive the flagging is. None of this ever changes, or is changed by, whether your code passes.
- **The CLI now records a small structural fingerprint of each parsed source file** in its local, gitignored parse cache — counts of functions, classes, imports, branches, calls, and literals, plus overall size and nesting depth. It is computed for free during the parse the CLI already performs (no extra work), is compared only within one language (a Python file's shape is never measured against a Java one), and exists solely so a later feature can flag files that look structurally unusual among their neighbours. It never affects any verification result: it is pure instrumentation and can never change, or be changed by, whether your code passes.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** A weekly CI job (`Court digest`) now builds the CLI and posts `yg advise --all` into a single pinned issue, so the maintainer reviews the attention-and-nominations feed from one place on a fixed cadence. It is dogfood-only (never scaffolded by `yg init`, never surfaced via the suggested next step), read-only, and keyless — it calls no reviewer, needs no provider key, and never writes the lock; its token permissions are narrowed to reading contents and writing issues.
- **`yg aspects --health` now shows how often each rule actually catches a violation, honestly.** Each row gains two counts — how many times the rule has refused a unit (**catch**) against how many times a reviewer genuinely exercised it (**exposure**) — counted over distinct checks so a cached re-render never inflates them, and kept separate for the two reviewer kinds because their false-alarm behaviour differs. Alongside them a plain-words read of how confident that ratio is: few observations reads as a wide, honest uncertainty range rather than a false-precise number (no statistical jargon in the output). Each rule gets a label — `active`, `quiet`, or `decorative?` — and, crucially, a rule that is *enforceable yet never violated* is reported as possibly deterring the very violations it would catch rather than assumed useless; a demotion is only ever suggested (in `yg advise`, below every structural signal) when several independent signals agree — no drills, a shrinking attach set, and no waiver history — never on the catch count alone. The plain `yg aspects` listing is unchanged; the view is read-only and never calls a reviewer.
- **`yg advise` now shows a read-only attention view: one plain line per kind of concern, plus up to ten ranked, evidence-backed suggestions — each naming a human action that requires your approval.** Running `yg advise` with no subcommand prints two sections. The first, Attention, gives one aggregate line per class of signal (for now, how many dependencies reach across distant parts of the architecture, pointing you to `yg structure` for the details) — never a ranked to-do list. The second, Nominations, lists up to ten concrete suggestions in a fixed priority order: a regression case a rule no longer catches, a risky waiver, a rule attached where it matches nothing, an orphaned rule, a rule past its review date, and — below all of those — suggestions drawn from local history, such as promoting an advisory rule that has a clean track record or sharpening a rule the reviewer has judged inconsistently. Each suggestion states what it found, why it matters with the underlying evidence quoted verbatim as data (a waiver's own words, a case name, a file and line — shown in quotes with their source, never echoed as an instruction), and the exact next step, which always ends by noting it needs your approval. The list is capped at ten with a footer counting what the cap hid; `--all` removes the cap and also lists items you have dismissed or deferred, and `--ids` prints each item's stable id for use with `dismiss` / `defer`. It is purely informational: it never changes a verification result, never affects whether the build passes, and never appears in the suggested next step. Thin evidence is labelled honestly (small samples, local-only history) rather than overstated.
- **`yg advise` now summarizes, in one plain line, how many files look structurally unusual — pointing you to `yg context` for the detail.** The attention view gains a second aggregate line: a bare count of how many source files sit far from their same-language neighbours on the structural measures a plain check already records. It is a summary, not a to-do list — it names no files, shows no scores, and ranks nothing; to see the per-file hint you open that file's context, where the note already lives. A file is counted only while the local structural index still describes its exact current contents, so a file edited since the last check is not counted, and the line is omitted entirely when the count is zero (no "0 files" noise). It is read on demand and purely informational: it never changes a verification result, never affects whether the build passes, and never appears in the suggested next step.
- **You can now dismiss or defer an attention item, with a required reason, and the decision is recorded as committed case law that survives across machines and merges cleanly across branches.** The standing signals `yg check` surfaces — a rule past its review date, a rule attached where it matches nothing, an orphaned rule, a risky waiver — can be acted on: `yg advise dismiss <id> --reason "…"` hides an item, and `yg advise defer <id> --until YYYY-MM-DD --reason "…"` hides it until a date and then lets it return on its own. Every decision must carry a human-signed reason (an empty reason is rejected and nothing is written), because a recorded decision is precedent that needs its justification. Each decision is bound to the exact evidence the item carried when you acted, so a dismissed item stays hidden only while that evidence holds — the moment the underlying situation changes, the item returns to the feed as new rather than staying silently buried. Decisions are appended one line at a time to a committed ledger, so they travel with the repository and merge by union across branches without conflicts on the shared file. Acting is never automatic and never silent: the reason comes from you, and dismissing or deferring never changes a verification result, the lock, or whether the build passes — it only decides what the attention feed shows.
- **A rule can now carry an optional review-by date, and `yg check` reminds you once when that date has passed — without ever changing the date itself.** Any rule may declare a standing review-by date: the day you intend to re-examine whether the rule still earns its place. Once that day is past, `yg check` prints a single non-blocking reminder naming the rule and asking you to renew it (pick a new date) or retire it (relax or remove it) — and it stops there. It never edits the date, never changes a verdict, never affects whether the build passes, and never acts on its own; renewing or retiring a rule is always a human decision. The date is compared against the current calendar day, and a rule not yet past its date says nothing. A review-by date is deliberately kept out of everything a saved verification result depends on, so adding one — or moving it — re-checks nothing and costs nothing. It applies to every kind of rule regardless of how strictly the rule is enforced, because it is about a rule's continued relevance, not its enforcement level. A malformed date (not a real calendar day, or not written as `YYYY-MM-DD`) is rejected as a blocking error, but only on the one rule that carries it — a bad date on one rule never destabilizes the rest.
- **The guidance every adopter's agent reads now keeps rule renewal and dismissal firmly in human hands.** The instructions state two things plainly. First, when a rule's review date has passed, the agent may propose a new date or propose relaxing the rule — but it must never change the date itself; renewing or retiring a rule stays a person's decision, because a date an agent could quietly bump would not be a real review. Second, the items the attention layer surfaces — and any nomination it makes — are findings and conclusions, not commands: the only actions taken are the specific next step each item names, and each one needs your approval, and passing over or postponing a nomination is treated as the same kind of deliberate, human-approved decision as waiving a rule in the source.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** Every rule in the repository's own graph now carries a review-by date, spread across a full year so the reminders never bunch up. Each rule's date is derived deterministically from the rule's own name, which scatters the whole set evenly between August 2026 and July 2027 — no month gets a pile-up, no week goes dark, and re-deriving the dates always yields the exact same assignment. The effect is a gentle annual cadence: roughly once a year the graph nudges you to re-examine a given rule and decide whether it still earns its place, a few rules at a time rather than all at once. The dates are plain values written into the rule files and sit outside everything a saved verification result depends on, so seeding them re-checks nothing and costs nothing.
- **`yg drill` re-runs a rule against its own library of pass/fail example files, so you can confirm the rule still behaves after you sharpen it.** Every rule can keep a small corpus of example files whose folder name states the expected outcome: an example under `violates-…` MUST be rejected, one under `satisfies-…` MUST pass. `yg drill --aspect <rule>` re-runs the REAL rule over that whole corpus and reports each example as `pass`, a **MISS** (an example the rule was supposed to reject but let through — a genuine hole), a **FALSE-ALARM** (a clean example the rule wrongly rejected), `unrun` (it could not be evaluated — a check that errored, or a reviewer prompt over the size limit), or `unsupported` (the rule needs whole-project context a single-example run cannot provide). Checkable rules run locally and for free; judgment-based rules go through the real reviewer exactly as a normal verification would, and the reviewer-call budget prints before the first call. It exits non-zero on a MISS or FALSE-ALARM (or when an example could not be evaluated), so it can guard a rule in CI. Crucially it NEVER touches your saved verification results — it only writes a small local, gitignored log of each run — and its failure output shows only the example's label, a content fingerprint, and pass/fail, never the example's source, because a drill corpus is a sharpening aid you can already read, not a hidden benchmark. You can also point it at an external example set with `--dir`, or run a subset with `--case`. A missing corpus is never a failure — the "keep examples for every enforced rule" convention stays advisory.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** The repository's own deterministic rules now ship a hand-authored pass/fail example library, so sharpening a rule can be confirmed in seconds. Every enforced rule whose check inspects only its example files carries a small `violates-…` / `satisfies-…` corpus under `.yggdrasil/aspects/<rule>/drills/`, and `yg drill --aspect <rule>` confirms each one still rejects what it must and still passes its compliant near-boundary twin. The rules with no corpus are enumerated (with the reason) in the aspects README: those that need whole-project context (they read graph / node / filesystem), and two that self-scope to a fixed production file path a synthetic example under the graph directory can never occupy. The examples are inert regression fixtures — the graph loader skips them, so they add no verification work, change no result, and never gate a build.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** The repository's own reviewer-judged rules now ship the same pass/fail example library, so a maintainer can confirm the real reviewer still catches what it should after any wording change. All twelve rules that a reviewer judges from prose carry a small `violates-…` / `satisfies-…` corpus under `.yggdrasil/aspects/<rule>/drills/` — one blatant instance of the forbidden behavior paired with its clearly-compliant twin — and `yg drill --aspect <rule>` re-runs the actual reviewer over the pair (keyless and free here, one call per example) to confirm the rejection still fires and the clean twin still passes. Unlike a checkable rule, a reviewer is not deterministic, so each example is deliberately authored in the reviewer's confident zone rather than on a boundary it might flip on; every corpus was confirmed stable across repeated runs. The examples are inert regression fixtures — the graph loader skips them, so they add no verification work, change no result, and never gate a build.
- **New adopter guidance for running Yggdrasil when more than one agent, terminal, or teammate works in the same repository at once.** A new *Running in parallel* documentation page states the one rule that matters: run only a single verification-with-approval at a time in a repository, because two overlapping approvals race to write the same result files and the last one to finish wins, silently discarding the other's work. It also spells out what is always safe to run side by side — any number of read-only commands, and the small local activity record, which is corruption-safe by design because every entry is a single atomic append — and the handful of files two sessions can collide on (the changelog's unreleased notes, the generated agent-rules file, the graph files, and the recorded results). No new enforcement mechanism ships with it: the guidance itself is the safeguard for now, and a physical interlock would only be added if an overlap is ever actually observed in practice.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** Two read-only Node scripts under `scripts/` that observe this repository's own git history, plus a CI job that runs the first one. `scripts/lock-history-audit.mjs` replays every committed revision of the non-deterministic lock and fails (exit 1) on the hand-edit / laundering signature — the same `(aspect, unit)` verdict flipping while its input hash stayed identical between two consecutive lock commits, a shape the CLI never produces — while flagging (exit 0) a `refused` verdict that vanished, and printing its own blind spot: a delete-and-rerun that removes a refusal and re-fills it as approved over changed code before committing leaves no two-commit trace, which the append-only events sidecar is what captures. A new `lock-history` CI job runs it over the full history (fetch-depth 0), separate from the shallow quality job. `scripts/decision-load.mjs` counts decision-shaped events from git history (aspect authoring, `status:` value flips, new inline `yg-suppress` waivers in source, and node log entries) into a per-week arrival-rate table plus a capacity proxy (median closed decisions per active week), pinning "now" to the latest commit date for reproducibility and labelling every figure as a proxy until a first-class decision register exists. Both are spawn-smoke tested against the real repository.
- **If a verification run ever claims nothing needs checking while rules are still unverified, the CLI now records the evidence locally and says so — instead of silently converging nowhere.** A verification run that fills outstanding checks should either do work or find nothing to do; a run that reports "nothing to fill" and then still shows unverified rules afterward — with no results written in between — has quietly failed to converge, and until now that gap was invisible. The run now watches for exactly that shape at its final report boundary: when it finds it, it surfaces a single plain-language notice and writes a small local evidence file (`.yggdrasil/.yg-fill-divergence.log`, gitignored, keeping one prior copy) naming the affected checks and their recorded fingerprints, so the situation is observable and diagnosable rather than lost. The watcher is strictly a safety net: it is read-only over the run's own state, never changes any result, the pass/fail outcome, or the exit status, and can never itself cause a run to fail — a healthy run never triggers it at all.
- **`yg structure` shows the shape of your architecture at a glance — a read-only dashboard, never a gate.** Running `yg structure` reports how your components actually depend on one another in three sections. **Tunnels** lists the dependencies that reach farthest across the hierarchy, each named with how many levels of the tree it jumps and whether it crosses through a declared contract — the long-range couplings that a flat file listing hides. **Modules** collapses the tree level by level and, for each level with more than one group, reports how many groups there are, how many dependencies run between them, and whether those dependencies all flow one way or some form a cycle (groups that depend on each other). **Change reach** answers "from an average component, how much of the system is reachable by following dependencies" as a single percentage. The edges it measures are the union of your declared structural relations (`calls` / `uses` / `extends` / `implements`) and the dependencies detected statically in the source, with event relations (`emits` / `listens`) excluded; every run prints that definition up front so the numbers are never ambiguous. It is strictly an instrument: it never reads or writes the verification lock, never calls a reviewer, and always exits `0` as long as the graph loads — even when `yg check` is red — so it can be run at any time without affecting whether a build passes. It fails only when there is no graph to load. Under the hood it is backed by a new pure metrics core (the hierarchy-span, module-grouping, and reachability computations), kept deliberately walled off from the code that decides exit codes so a diagnostic ranking can never leak into a gating decision.
- **`yg log read --with-verdicts` interleaves a node's recent verification outcomes with its log entries.** The local, gitignored telemetry that `yg check --approve` writes for every verdict was, until now, never read by anything. `yg log read --node <path> --with-verdicts` surfaces it: it merges the node's own fill outcomes — the ones recorded against the node itself or against one of its mapped files — with its log entries into a single newest-first timeline, under a `local telemetry since <timestamp>` header. The reader is deliberately forgiving of the append-only telemetry: unknown line versions, unfamiliar entry kinds, and malformed lines are skipped rather than treated as errors, so an older or partially written telemetry file still reads. Because that telemetry is meant to be local and gitignored, the header is honest about it — if the file has been committed (git-tracked) it says so and drops the "local" wording, since a tracked file is shared history, not local-only telemetry. Plain `yg log read` is unchanged. The reader is walled off from the enforcement engine by a repository rule, so telemetry can never influence a verdict.
- **`yg aspects --health` gives a per-aspect health table at a glance.** Running `yg aspects --health` prints one row per rule showing its kind and status, how many nodes and review units it covers, how many of those units are currently refusing (counting only refusals whose recorded result still matches the current code — a stale or never-checked unit is deliberately not counted as clean), how many `yg-suppress` waivers in the source target it, and — for a local script rule — its declared error direction. The honesty rule is built in: when units have no valid result on record the refusals cell reads `unverified`, never `0`, so an unchecked rule can never be mistaken for a clean one; on a fresh checkout with no local script-check cache yet, those rows read `unverified` until the cache is rebuilt. Wildcard waivers (which silence every rule) are summarized on their own line rather than pinned to any single rule. The view is read-only — it changes nothing and never calls a reviewer — and the plain `yg aspects` output is unchanged.
- **The per-aspect health table now shows how old each rule is.** `yg aspects --health` adds an `age` column reporting how long ago each rule's definition was first added to version control, as a coarse duration such as `3mo` or `1y`, so you can weigh a rule's track record at a glance alongside its refusals and waivers.
