When that history is unavailable — a shallow clone, or a working copy with no repository — the cell reads `unknown` rather than a fabricated `0`, so a rule with no readable history is never mistaken for a brand-new one. The lookup runs only in this health view; the plain `yg aspects` listing is untouched and does no extra work.
- **A deterministic rule can now declare its error direction, and a waiver that silences a false-positive-free rule is flagged.** A deterministic check can be labeled with how it can be wrong: `over` (it may flag code the rule does not actually forbid — false positives possible), `under` (it only ever fires on provable violations — no false positives by design), or `exact` (neither). The label is optional, allowed only on deterministic rules, validated when present (a bad value or a label on a reviewer-judged rule is a blocking error naming the fix), and is pure metadata — labeling or relabeling it never re-verifies anything, because it is never part of what a stored verdict is keyed on. Building on it, the suppression inventory now warns when a waiver targets an `under`-labeled rule: such a rule produces no false positives to begin with, so silencing one is almost always either a mislabel or a real problem worth a second look. The warning is advisory — the inventory still exits cleanly.
- **The local fill event log now records which judge produced each LLM verdict.** Each event line the fill appends for an LLM-reviewed pair now carries the provider and model that judged it, so a later analysis of that local telemetry can separate one judge regime from another — which model refused often, or whether a verdict came from a reviewer that has since been swapped out. A rule can be silently re-pointed to a different reviewer tier without changing anything the lock records, so without this identity the telemetry would mix judge regimes with no way to tell them apart. The provider and model are written only on LLM lines — a deterministic check has no judge — and lines written before this change carry no judge and read as "regime unknown". As before, this is local-only telemetry: gitignored, never committed, and never read back by any check/verify/render path — and the judge identity is pure verdict metadata, never part of any content hash.
- **A component that is a deliberate single-responsibility seam can now declare a higher, justified limit for how many other components it may directly depend on.** Yggdrasil warns when one component depends on too many others, because sprawling dependencies usually mean unclear responsibilities. But some components are *meant* to be a single hub — one auditable gateway to a subsystem, or one orchestrator that coordinates a whole operation — and splitting them to satisfy the warning would defeat their purpose. Such a component can now record its own, higher ceiling directly in its definition, together with a written reason for it. The reason is required: the higher limit cannot be set without stating why, so the exception is explicit and reviewable rather than a silent, blanket loosening. The default stays strict for every other component, and the component still gets warned if it grows past the number it declared — so the allowance sanctions one specific, reviewed count, it does not turn the warning off. The declared number and its justification show up wherever you inspect the component, and a partial or malformed declaration is ignored (the strict default applies). It only affects the warning; it changes nothing about how anything is verified.
- **Diagnostic re-runs of a rule now record local telemetry, and you can dry-fit a rule under a different reviewer before switching models.** When you re-run a rule to gauge how consistently it judges the same code, each run now leaves a small local, gitignored note recording which reviewer judged the code and how it voted — the raw material for later "how stable is this rule?" and "how would a different model rule on this?" analysis that was, until now, never captured. And a new option lets you re-run the exact same checks under any named reviewer from your configuration instead of the one the rule normally uses — the dry-fit for "does this still pass under the model I'm about to switch to?" — so you can compare a candidate reviewer against the current one before committing to the swap. Both are purely diagnostic: they never change your architecture description, never touch your saved verification results, and never gate a build; the telemetry is write-only observability that nothing in a normal check ever reads back. Naming a reviewer that does not exist is a clear error listing the ones that do.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** The build now blocks any first-party source file that contains a raw control byte — a literal NUL (`0x00`) or any other non-printing C0 control character except tab, newline, and carriage return. Such a byte is invisible to type-checking, linting, and tests, and makes git treat the file as binary, so the file never diffs and the corruption slips silently past review — a defect that had reached this repository three separate times before being caught by eye. A new enforced deterministic rule closes that gap: it scans every source file across the CLI source, the build/CI scripts, and the test suite (the same file types where the defect struck), runs locally at zero cost with no API key, and refuses a file the instant it finds such a byte, pointing at the exact offset. It has no false positives by design — the legitimate way to put a NUL in a string is the escape `\0`, which is ordinary text and passes — so the guard itself is subject to the guard (its own definition and example files are clean). Documentation-only and configuration-only files (Markdown, YAML) are out of scope for this first version.
- **`yg check` now warns when a rule is attached where it can never apply.** A new non-blocking warning fires when a rule that ships an actual reviewer definition, and is not parked as a work-in-progress, ends up applying to no component at all — after every channel by which a rule can reach a component and after all of its conditional applicability predicates. Such a rule looks enforced but is never verified anywhere ("dead law that looks enforced"), which almost always signals a graph-authoring slip: a predicate that matches nothing, or an attachment aimed at a component or type that does not exist yet. The check reuses the very same effectiveness computation the reviewer uses to decide where rules apply, so it can never disagree with what actually gets verified. It stays completely silent while the model has no components yet, so a design-first workflow (rules authored before the code they govern) is never spammed, and it ignores bundle-style rules that carry no reviewer definition of their own (an unused bundle is already reported separately). Being a warning, it never fails a check run.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface.)** Fail-closed coverage extended to two reviewer-machinery faults that were only proven at the unit/runner level, not end-to-end at the fill boundary. A subprocess test now pins that an LLM rule whose supporting reference file is present-but-unreadable at prompt-assembly time (a regular file the graph validator accepts, then made unreadable) fails CLOSED: the fill writes nothing for the pair, never bills the reviewer, and the run stays red — the reference-file analogue of the existing unreadable-subject-file guarantee, and distinct from the missing/directory reference the graph validator already rejects. A second test pins that a local check whose module cannot even be imported (an unresolvable top-level import) is treated at the free, keyless deterministic fill as an infrastructure fault, not a code refusal: the pair is left unverified with no result written and a later plain check stays red — the load-time counterpart to the already-covered call-time crash. No product behavior changed; both faults were already fail-closed in the engine, and these tests are the standing proof.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** Four read-only Node scripts under `scripts/` that turn this repository's own local, gitignored telemetry (the verdict-events and drill-results sidecars) plus git history into offline calibration analyses. None writes the lock or any telemetry, none calls a reviewer, none is wired into `yg` — each is a standalone `node scripts/<x>.mjs`, filters events by their `source` discriminator so it never mixes regimes, and ends every run with a mandatory honesty footer (`local telemetry since <first timestamp>` — or a REFUSAL of that label if the sidecar has been committed to git — plus `small-N` and `unknown ≠ zero`). A missing or garbage telemetry file yields an honest empty report and exit 0, never a crash. `scripts/judge-stability.mjs` measures reviewer **self-consistency**: it groups repeated runs on identical input (diagnostic re-runs and repeated drill cases) and reports any k/N split where the reviewer disagreed with itself — measured rule ambiguity, not correctness — nominating the rule for a sharpened `content.md`; deterministic checks are excluded (consistent by construction) and tracked only as a non-determinism sanity channel. `scripts/cusum.mjs` runs a per-rule Bernoulli **CUSUM** over the fill stream (refused = 1, approved = 0, infra excluded) to detect a *sustained* upward shift in a rare refusal rate — the right tool where a p-chart is not — alarming only on a cluster the baseline says should be near-zero, with documented, tunable control parameters printed up front. `scripts/mcnemar.mjs --old <tier> --new <tier>` runs a paired **McNemar** comparison of two reviewer tiers over the same drill cases (exact binomial for small discordance, else the continuity-corrected chi-square), the honest before/after for a model swap; single-tier data reports "need two tiers to compare" and exits 0. `scripts/displacement.mjs` runs the Bode **"waterbed"** analysis — after a rule's source is sharpened, it measures the refusal-rate delta of that rule's siblings (rules sharing a verification subject) in a window around the edit, treating the *absence* of displacement as a result as publishable as its presence, and a window with no decided trials as unknown rather than zero. All four are spawn-smoke tested plus fixture-backed for both their signal and honest-empty paths.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** `scripts/metamorphic.mjs` (analysis e), an offline **metamorphic probe** that rewrites a rule's own example code in MEANING-PRESERVING ways and checks whether the LLM reviewer's verdict wobbles — measuring rule/judge **consistency, not correctness** (§10). For a rule it pairs a `satisfies-…` example (expected: satisfied) with its `violates-…` counterpart (expected: refused) and generates surface variants of each: two INVARIANT transforms that must preserve the verdict (an AST-scoped identifier rename and a whitespace normalization) and treats the `violates-…` file as the COVARIANT counterpart that must stay refused even under that surface noise. The rename is a genuine alpha-rename, never a regex substitution: it parses with the CLI's own tree-sitter grammars, renames only a locally-bound `identifier` (never an import/export name, property key, or shorthand-fused property), uniformly across the file, to a fresh name that collides with no existing identifier — and reports "no safe rename" rather than risk changing meaning; the reformat leaves string/template/comment spans byte-identical and is deterministic and idempotent. Each variant is judged by the REAL reviewer through `yg aspect-test` against a throwaway one-file temp fixture that is created and deleted per run — ZERO writes to the real lock, telemetry, or graph, and nothing folds into any verdict hash. It reports per rule `k/m invariant transforms preserved the verdict; j/n covariant cases stayed refused`, with `incomplete`/`error` reviewer runs excluded from every count (unknown ≠ zero) and the same small-N / inconsistency-not-correctness honesty footer as the other instruments. The default (no-arg) mode is an OFFLINE transform self-check (parse + rename + reformat + determinism/idempotency proofs, no reviewer calls) — the CI/spawn smoke; `--run` executes the pilot against the live reviewer. Shipped with a pilot on two LLM rules (`what-why-next`, `diagnostic-logging`) plus a determinism/collision/span unit suite over the pure transforms and a spawn-smoke of the offline mode. Documented as a companion trust technique in the model-swap protocol.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** `scripts/escape-scan.mjs`, the first seed of the "reality oracle" — an offline, read-only scan that surfaces likely *escapes*: fix commits that repaired code a rule had already approved green. It walks the mainline git history for fix-flavored commits (a conservative, documented vocabulary — `fix`/`fixes`/`fixed`/`fixup`/`bug`/`bugs`/`bugfix`/`hotfix`/`regression`, matched whole-word, which also covers the conventional `fix(scope):` form) that touched a graph node's mapped source files, and for each reads the PARENT commit's committed non-deterministic lock via `git show <parent>:<lock>` to ask whether that node was GREEN at the time — it had at least one committed verdict there and none of them was a refusal. CI-green is approximated by "the parent is on the mainline first-parent chain" (a full CI-status join would need the GitHub Actions API, out of scope for an offline scan). When all three hold, the commit is listed as an escape CANDIDATE for human triage — never a gate, never a metric — alongside an auditable per-outcome breakdown (candidates, and each exclusion reason: parent off the first-parent chain, no committed lock at the parent, node uncovered/unverified there, or the healthy case where the parent lock already refused the node). Node ownership is resolved with a minimal read of the current graph's `mapping:` globs rather than a heavy graph-loader import. It makes ZERO reviewer calls, writes NOTHING (never the lock, telemetry, or drill-results), and is not wired into `yg` — a standalone `node scripts/escape-scan.mjs`. Every run ends with two mandatory honesty labels: it UNDERCOUNTS (the deterministic verdict lock is local + gitignored, hence retroactively invisible in history, so the scan sees only LLM refusals, and the live relation-conformance check leaves no lock trace) and it OVERCOUNTS (not every fix-commit fixes a defect). Spawn-smoke tested against the real repository plus a fixture-backed synthetic-history proof of all three join outcomes.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** `scripts/spectral-headroom.mjs`, an offline structural report that answers one question in plain language: how much tighter is the codebase's *natural* module boundary than the directory layout it actually ships? It reads the SAME structural dependency graph the `yg structure` dashboard reports — declared structural relations (`calls`/`uses`/`extends`/`implements`) unioned with statically detected code dependencies, event relations excluded — through the very computation the dashboard runs, so the two can never drift; it makes ZERO reviewer calls, writes NOTHING (no lock, no telemetry), and is not wired into `yg` — a standalone `node scripts/spectral-headroom.mjs`. It projects that graph to an undirected simple graph, keeps the largest connected piece, and finds the tightest natural split (the minimum-conductance cut along the graph's Fiedler ordering — its second-eigenvalue direction) computed by fully deterministic, machine-independent math (a fixed seed, a fixed iteration count, and sorted tie-breaks, so the output is byte-identical on any host and across runs). It then measures the conductance of each actual top-level-directory cut and reports the headroom — how many times looser the best directory-aligned boundary is than the natural one — with a per-directory table. Crucially it ships with the honesty label built into every run: conductance rewards balanced cuts, so a small cohesive module legitimately scores low — these are candidates for human eyes, never defects. Proven on a hand-checkable two-triangles-joined-by-one-edge fixture (with the eigenvalue independently cross-checked) and spawn-smoke + determinism tested against the real repository.
- **New adopter documentation on safely swapping the model behind a reviewer.** A *Model-Swap Protocol* page explains why changing the model a reviewer tier points at is silent by design — a saved verification result depends only on the tier's *name*, never on the model behind it, so re-pointing a tier leaves every existing green result untouched and now attributed to a model that never actually reviewed that code — and gives the procedure to make the change visible before it becomes a silent regression: stand the candidate up as a separate tier, run the same example cases through both models, and compare them head-to-head with a paired statistical test that only weighs the cases where the two models disagree. It spells out how to read the outcome as a decision (interchangeable, quietly weaker, or stricter) and reminds you that after the swap the old green results are stale opinions you must deliberately re-verify if it matters.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** A new advisory rule now warns when one of the shipped example projects reaches outside its own folder. Each example under `examples/` is meant to be copy-and-run self-contained — a maintainer can lift any one folder out and it still works — so the rule flags a source file that reaches across a sibling example's directory: a relative `import`/`export … from`, `require`, or dynamic `import` in a JS/TS-family file whose target resolves outside the example's own folder. It only ever fires on a provable cross-directory reference (a Python relative import or a computed specifier is left alone), so it has no false positives, warns without ever blocking, and costs nothing to evaluate. It ships a pass/fail example corpus.
- **(Repo-internal — not an adopter surface, never scaffolded by `yg init`.)** Two new advisory drift-protection rules now watch this repository's own CI and quality gate, and both warn without ever blocking a build. The first warns when a GitHub Actions step pulls in an external action without pinning it to a version, so the gate the build relies on cannot silently change under a floating branch — a tag or SHA pin (`@v4`, `@<sha>`), a local `./` action, a `docker://` action, and a reusable-workflow call all pass. The second warns when the repository's single gate script drops one of its expected check categories — typecheck, lint, build, test/coverage, docs build, markdownlint, or the graph check — so a whole class of regression cannot slip through because a step was quietly removed. Both are content scans (the workflow files are YAML and the gate is a shell script, neither with a parse tree here), so they over-approximate and are deliberately advisory. Deleting the gate step from CI entirely is left uncovered by design and documented as such: these protect against drift, not tampering. Both ship a pass/fail example corpus and cost nothing to evaluate.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood + example showcase.)** This repository and its six shipped example projects now install and enforce the universal agent-rules distribution on themselves — each carries the hash-anchored `AGENTS.md` digest, the `CLAUDE.md` import, and `.clinerules/yggdrasil.md`. `scripts/repo-check.sh` gained a digest-freshness gate covering the repo root and every example: a pure hash comparison against the canonical digest the built CLI would produce, run before the graph check and never shelling out to `yg check` inside an example (one of the six is deliberately red, to demonstrate a refusal, and must stay that way). Each example's coverage configuration was read individually before editing rather than edited uniformly: two already excluded everything outside `src/` and needed no change, two required `src/` with only `README.md` excluded and had the new files added to that same excluded list, and two had no coverage block at all — defaulting to requiring the whole tree — and gained one excluding the new files.

### Changed

- **`yg prime` now participates in a flow, completing the flow map.** It had a component and full rule coverage but belonged to no flow, so it was missing from the process picture `yg flows` and the portal's flow view draw — the only place a command's membership is visible. It joins the reference-browsing flow alongside the two other commands that serve content bundled in the CLI, need no graph present, and write nothing; that flow's description now states the shared shape.
- **Refreshed dependencies.** Runtime (`web-tree-sitter`, `ignore`, `brace-expansion`) and dev/build tooling (`eslint`, `prettier`, `vitest`, `@vitest/coverage-v8`, `typescript-eslint`, `@types/node`) moved to current within-range versions, and CI `setup-node` to v7. No behavior change; `typescript` intentionally held at 6.x (the ecosystem does not yet support TypeScript 7).
- **`yg init` now installs one universal set of agent-rules artifacts for every agent at once, instead of asking which platform you use.** A fresh bootstrap writes the same three files: a hash-anchored summary inside marker comments in `AGENTS.md`, a `@AGENTS.md` import line added to `CLAUDE.md` (Claude Code does not read `AGENTS.md` on its own), and a copy of the same summary at `.clinerules/yggdrasil.md` (Cline looks there instead); on an already-adopted repo they are refreshed only on request — `yg init --upgrade`, or the interactive menu's refresh option. There is no platform question left to answer, in the interactive wizard or anywhere else, and `yg init --upgrade` no longer requires naming one. `--platform <name>` is still accepted everywhere it used to be, purely for backward compatibility, and always prints a deprecation notice: on a fresh repo the run then proceeds exactly as if the flag had been dropped, whatever value it was given; on an already-adopted repo, though, passing it non-interactively still triggers the same rules refresh it always did, deliberately, so a script that used to pass it keeps working unchanged. A bare, non-interactive `yg init` (Docker, a devcontainer, or CI with no reviewer flag given) now performs a keyless universal bootstrap the same way naming a platform alone used to.
- **Upgrading an existing project adds four files at its root — and a project that requires its whole tree to be mapped needs one line of configuration for them.** `yg init --upgrade` writes `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `.clinerules/yggdrasil.md`, and maintains a `.gitattributes` entry. On a project whose `yg-config.yaml` has no `coverage:` block at all — or whose required roots cover the repository root — every tracked file must belong to a component, so those four become blocking "unmapped" errors on the very next check. They are repository plumbing rather than project source; exclude them from coverage:

  ```yaml
  coverage:
    excluded:
      - AGENTS.md
      - CLAUDE.md
      - .clinerules/
      - .gitattributes
  ```

  (Mapping them to a component instead works equally well.) `yg init --upgrade` now detects this situation and prints exactly that stanza, naming the files it applies to; it reports only and never edits your configuration, since which files a project holds under enforcement is not the command's decision to make. A freshly initialized project requires nothing and is unaffected. The adopter guides — getting started, platforms, and the coverage reference — carry the same note.
- **`yg init` sweeps away every artifact a retired per-platform installer used to write.** Agent rules used to be written per platform — thirteen different installers, each writing or appending to a different file (`.cursor/rules/yggdrasil.mdc`, `.github/copilot-instructions.md`, `.roo/rules/yggdrasil.md`, `.windsurf/rules/yggdrasil.md`, an added `read:` entry in `.aider.conf.yml`, an import line in `GEMINI.md`, the old `.yggdrasil/agent-rules.md`, an appended `AGENTS.md` section from the Codex/OpenCode installers, and Amp's own `AGENTS.md` import line, among others). `yg init` and `yg init --upgrade` now remove whichever of these a project still carries, reported as "Legacy per-platform artifacts cleaned up" in the command's output, so upgrading from an older CLI never leaves a mix of stale per-platform files alongside the new universal ones.

- **(Repo-internal.)** The repository's own quality gate now checks the digest anchor in both committed agent-rules artifacts at every project root it visits — its own and each shipped example — rather than only the first. It had been inspecting strictly less than the warning-level check it backstops, which compares both; a stale second artifact could have shipped past a green gate.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Removed a fully orphaned type-only module — a single interface left behind, with no importers anywhere, when the per-layer context builder was retired — along with the now-empty graph node it was mapped to and two dangling dependency declarations that pointed at that node. Verified zero references across the whole tree first.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Narrowed the internal surface of several modules: about two dozen helper functions and constants used only within their own file were carrying an unnecessary `export`, overstating each module's public interface. Dropped the keyword so the exported surface reflects only what other modules actually consume; verified no other module or test referenced any of them, and behavior is unchanged.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Corrected four stale internal doc comments that named functions or behavior no longer present after earlier refactors (a removed drift-baseline cycle handler, two renamed path-resolver helpers, and a miscount of which callers write the feature-field index), so they no longer point a future reader at code that is not there. Comment-only.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Retired a dead "layer builder" subsystem in the context-data builder — six exported per-layer helper functions with no production callers (the context builders were refactored to assemble their output directly and no longer used them; they survived only because a dedicated test exercised them). Removed the functions, the types and imports they alone used, and their now-orphaned tests, keeping the live context-data builders and their tests unchanged.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Removed four dead, unreferenced exports (a derived provider type, a retired graph-file-path helper, an unused filesystem stat helper, and an unused lock-diagnostic message builder) along with the imports they left dangling. Verified zero importers across the whole tree first.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Deduplicated the provider→API-key-env-var map in the init reviewer setup — the interactive key prompt now reads the single `API_KEY_ENV` source of truth instead of re-declaring the same table inline.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Removed a dead migration-runner function whose applicable-filter / sort / run logic was fully duplicated by, and superseded by, the live version-upgrade runner. It had no production callers; the live runner's own tests already cover the same ordering and skip behavior.
- **(Repo-internal.)** The language-registry module (the pure extension-to-grammar lookup with no dependencies of its own) moved from the core layer to the utility leaf, so the parsing, relation-extraction, and structural-check layers no longer reach up into core to use it — cutting a two-level dependency cycle. No behavior change; the table and its helpers are byte-identical.
- **(Repo-internal.)** Widened the test suite's branch-coverage headroom so the hard 90%-on-every-metric gate no longer sits on a cliff edge. Branch coverage was 0.55 points above the floor, close enough that ordinary run-to-run variance could tip the gate red on a clean tree. Added 114 genuine tests — real on-disk fixtures, no mocking of core logic — covering previously-untested branches (the attention/nomination feed, the input-hash and companion-resolution paths, the structure context-parser and I/O leaves, and several language extractors' error-recovery arms), lifting branch coverage to 92.0% and every other metric with it. Test-only; no product code changed, and two consecutive coverage runs agreed to the branch.

- **A proposed option to have the reviewer report how confident it was in each judgment was evaluated against the repository's known-answer review cases and NOT adopted.** The idea was to record the reviewer's own confidence score alongside each result and later check whether that score tracked whether the reviewer had actually been right. It did not carry usable signal: the reviewer reported a near-constant, uniformly high confidence on every case, and the known-answer set — visible fixtures that exist to catch regressions — contains no cases the reviewer gets wrong, so there was nothing for a confidence score to distinguish. Asking for the score changed no judgment and always read back cleanly, so the option is cheap and harmless and could be revisited on a set that genuinely includes cases the reviewer sometimes misjudges; but for now no confidence score is collected, and nothing about how verification works changes.

- **This repository's own architecture now attaches its two baseline source-hygiene rules — POSIX-only path separators, and routing every glob match through the single shared engine — through one named bundle instead of two hand-repeated lists maintained per node type.** The two lists had quietly drifted apart: five kinds of source file carried only the glob-engine rule and had lost the path-separator rule, purely because the parallel lists were edited by hand at different times, not by intent. Collapsing both rules into one reusable bundle attached in a single place closes that gap — those five kinds of file now carry both rules again — and removes the duplicated bookkeeping that let them diverge in the first place. This is an internal maintenance change to how Yggdrasil enforces its own codebase; no CLI behavior changes for adopters, and every previously recorded verification result is preserved unchanged.

- **The focused-file-exports guidance is confirmed as an intentional advisory signal — a soft single-responsibility hint that surfaces as a warning rather than blocking, not a rule slated for enforcement.** It is an over-approximating heuristic kept advisory by design, so it never wrongly blocks a file that legitimately exports several symbols; it stays a non-blocking hint rather than gating a build.

- **The local record of verification outcomes now distinguishes which activity produced each entry, so later analysis never mixes them.** Yggdrasil keeps a private, gitignored record of every verification result so that otherwise-invisible outcomes — a refusal, or an infrastructure failure that silently blocks a run — stay observable across runs. Each entry now carries a label naming which of three activities wrote it: a routine verification pass, an on-demand deep-dive re-run of a single rule, or a diagnostic re-run that gauges how consistently a rule judges. Those activities repeat and select work differently, so pooling their entries together would misstate how often a rule actually refuses; the label lets a reader keep the three apart. The label is descriptive metadata only — it is never part of what decides whether a stored result is still valid, so adding it re-verifies nothing, and entries written before this change are read as coming from the routine pass. All three activities now tag their own entries — the routine verification pass, the on-demand single-rule re-run, and the diagnostic re-run. No existing entry or outcome changes.

- **Documented why an auto-approving check can, on rare occasions, disagree with itself within a single run — and confirmed it is not a defect.** On a repository with auto-approval turned on, a lone check both fills outstanding rules and reports on them in one pass. If a background tool rewrites a tracked file in the moment between those two steps — for example a package manager pinning a version field in the project manifest, a formatter, or a code generator — the two steps read different versions of that file and the run can flag the just-touched rule as unverified, even though a plain re-run over the now-settled file passes. The verification itself is deterministic on unchanging files and never writes your source, so this only ever stems from an outside writer touching a file mid-run, which Yggdrasil cannot prevent; it fails safe (never reports green over bytes it did not see) and records the small local note it already writes for this exact situation. The *Running in parallel* guide and the configuration reference now describe this case and its one-step fix: let the background tool finish, then re-run.

- **`yg suppressions` no longer flags a deliberate whole-file waiver placed at the top of a file as an "Unbounded range".** A bare disable marker with no matching enable waives everything below it to the end of the file — the sanctioned way to exempt an entire file. Until now the inventory warned "Unbounded range" on every such marker, including the intentional top-of-file form, which made a deliberate whole-file waiver indistinguishable from a mid-file marker whose closing enable was simply forgotten. The inventory now classifies a bare disable that sits within the first five lines of a file that contain any non-whitespace text (blank lines do not count, but a shebang and each line of a header comment do) as `file-level`: it is listed under that label and draws no warning. A bare disable placed lower still reads as "Unbounded range", since there it usually is a forgotten close. This is a classification-and-reporting change only — what each reviewer actually waives is byte-for-byte unchanged (the resolved suppressed line ranges are identical for every reviewer kind), and `yg suppressions` still exits cleanly.

- **The names of the three agent-rules files Yggdrasil installs are now defined in one place inside the CLI, and a rule keeps them there.** The digest host file, the file that imports it, and the standalone Cline copy were each spelled out independently by the installer, by the reader that judges whether they are current, and by the code that labels findings about them. That duplication had already produced a real defect once: the reader looked for one fixed spelling while the installer wrote to whichever casing a repository already used, so a repository whose file was cased differently was reported as having no rules installed at all — a warning that re-running the installer could never clear. The three now share one definition, and an enforced rule refuses any file in the CLI's own source that types one of those names out again instead of using the shared one, so a fourth place cannot quietly reintroduce the same divergence. Nothing an adopter sees changes: the files written, their names, their casing resolution, and their line endings are byte-for-byte identical.

### Security

- **Resolved a high-severity path-traversal advisory in a transitive dependency (`postcss`).** Bumped `postcss` to 8.5.23 in both the CLI and the docs site; the advisory (arbitrary `.map` file disclosure via source-map auto-loading) no longer affects the shipped or built artifacts.
- **A suppression example inside a code block in a Windows-line-ending Markdown file is no longer honored as a real waiver.** Yggdrasil shields documented suppression examples — the ones that appear in rule-authoring docs — from being read as live "turn this rule off" directives by masking anything inside a Markdown code fence. On a Markdown file saved with Windows (carriage-return) line endings, that mask silently came back empty: the fence was never recognized, so every example marker inside a code block switched off enforcement for the line beneath it. A project edited on Windows that brings such a doc file under a component would quietly lose enforcement wherever an example sat. The mask now handles carriage-return line endings identically to Unix ones, so a documented example can never leak out as a live waiver. (Unix-line-ending files were never affected.)
- **A crafted component directory name can no longer inject shell commands when reconciling logs after a merge.** Reconciling a component's history after a merge reads earlier versions of its log file out of git, and the component's own path was spliced into a shell command string — so a component directory whose name contained shell syntax (which a merged change could introduce) could run arbitrary commands on the machine of anyone who then ran the reconcile step, a step the agent instructions tell an assistant to run automatically after a merge. Every git call on this path now passes its arguments directly to git with no shell involved, so a path is always a literal argument and can never be interpreted as a command. Behaviour is otherwise unchanged.
- **A missing rule file (or an unreadable one) can no longer let the check pass over unenforced code.** A malformed applicability filter on a rule — for example confusing the two filter grammars, or an empty clause — used to escape as an uncaught error that the rule loader silently absorbed, dropping that rule and every rule loaded after it while the check still reported a clean pass. Such a filter is now reported as a blocking error naming the offending rule, and the loader only treats a genuinely absent rules directory as empty — any other failure is surfaced, never swallowed. This closes a path by which a simple rule-authoring typo could silently disable enforcement.
- **The local architecture map's approve action can no longer be triggered by another website.** The optional local web view (`yg portal`) answers a few requests that do real work — most importantly the one approve action that runs verification. Those requests were served to anything that reached the local address, and because a browser will send a request to a local address from any page you have open — even though it cannot read the answer back — a malicious site open in another tab could quietly trigger the approve action, or repeatedly launch the cost preview, without the operator's knowledge. Serving only on the local machine does not stop this, because the request comes from the operator's own browser. The portal now answers those working requests only when they carry a marker its own page attaches (something another site cannot add) and rejects any request that claims to come from a different address, so the view stays reachable exactly as before for the person running it while a cross-site trigger is refused.
- **A component mapping that points outside the repository can no longer reach a reviewer.** A mapping entry that climbed above the project root with `..` (or an absolute path) was not rejected, and the approval run then read that out-of-project file and included its contents in the prompt sent to the configured reviewer — a path-traversal that could exfiltrate a file from outside the repo to an external reviewer service. Such an entry is now refused the moment the component file is parsed (the component never loads), and two further guards back it up: the approval run's pre-flight gate stops before reading any file, and the file-expansion step refuses to return a path outside the project root. An in-repo `..` that resolves back inside the project (`a/../b`) is still allowed.

### Fixed

- **(Repo-internal tooling.)** Two dogfood analyses read the verdict-fill stream from the local sidecar only, so once `events.committed_llm` was enabled they were analysing a series that stopped at the moment of the flip while still reporting it as current — `cusum` raised one alarm from a truncated series where the full one yields four. Both now mirror the CLI's own reader: union of the local sidecar, its rotation, and the committed stream, deduped by full line so a `merge=union` duplicate cannot inflate a trial count. Their honesty footers name which files were read, which were absent, and that a series drawn partly from the shared stream spans machines and judge regimes.
- **(Repo-internal tooling.)** The quality gate's failure line no longer reads as a coverage threshold miss when the actual cause was a failing test. `scripts/repo-check.sh` ran the coverage test suite and a separate coverage-threshold assertion as two independent steps; when a test failed, the coverage step still ran — against a coverage summary that was never produced, or reflected only a partial run — and failed too, so the final failure line listed both steps with nothing to tell a maintainer which had actually gone wrong. This happened during recent work: a single flaky test read as a coverage regression and cost a real investigation to correctly diagnose. The coverage assertion now runs only when the test step it depends on actually succeeded; when the test step fails, the coverage step reports itself as skipped — naming the test step as the cause — rather than presenting as a second, unrelated threshold miss. That first fix keyed the friendly message on *why* the coverage file might be missing (the test step failed) rather than on *whether the file is actually usable* — so if the test step passes but `coverage-summary.json` is absent, unparsable, or missing the totals the assertion reads (a misconfigured reporter, `json-summary` dropped from the reporter list, `coverage/` cleaned between steps), the step still crashed with a raw `MODULE_NOT_FOUND` stack trace under the bare label `CLI: coverage >= 90%` — the exact "coverage regressed" misreading the first fix existed to eliminate. The coverage step now checks, before reading any threshold, that the summary file exists, parses as JSON, and carries the `total.{lines,statements,functions,branches}.pct` fields the assertion needs; if not, it fails with a distinct message stating plainly that this is not a threshold miss and naming the likely causes, while still failing the build. The threshold logic and its success output are unchanged.
- **A repository that documents its own agent setup can no longer end up looking installed while giving Claude Code nothing.** Setup adds a one-line `@AGENTS.md` import to `CLAUDE.md`, and both the writer and `yg check` recognized that line anywhere it appeared — including inside a fenced code block, i.e. in an example the repository was merely showing its readers. A `CLAUDE.md` whose only such line was a quoted example was therefore left untouched while the command reported success, and the check reported the import present; the two agreed with each other and both were wrong, so nothing anywhere signalled that the agent was getting no rules at all. Both sides now use the one line scanner that already ignores fenced examples for the digest block, so a documented example is never mistaken for a live setting — and the same is true of the retired rules-file import the upgrade sweeps away, which is likewise no longer deleted out of an example.
- **A file with mixed line endings is no longer rewritten end to end.** When setup edits `AGENTS.md` or `CLAUDE.md` it writes the file back in the line ending that file uses. It decided that on the presence of a single Windows line ending anywhere in the file, so a file that was otherwise entirely Unix but carried one stray Windows ending came back fully converted — every line changed in the diff, for a one-line edit. It now keeps whichever ending the file mostly uses.
- **The `read:` key an old setup left in an Aider configuration is no longer removed too eagerly.** Removing the entry a retired installer wrote also removed any now-empty `read:` key — but it looked at every such key in the file, so a separate, unrelated empty `read:` key the user had written themselves could be deleted as collateral. It now only removes the key its own entry actually sat under, and only when nothing else is left beneath it.
- **`yg check` no longer reports a component that does not exist when the committed agent-rules digest has drifted.** That warning is about three files at the repository root, not about any component, but it was attached to a placeholder component name — so the grouped report printed a count of "1 nodes" and listed a component you cannot open, the per-component summary gave it a row of its own, and the local web view listed it among a group's components with no page behind it. Findings that belong to the repository rather than to a component now render without the component framing entirely, in every view.
- **An agent on a platform with a smaller instruction budget no longer silently works from truncated rules.** The generated rules file used to be written whole into a project (around 53 KB for this repository's own manual), and at least one adopted agent platform's own instruction budget is smaller than that — OpenAI Codex caps project instructions at 32 KiB — so the file was silently cut off partway through, and the agent worked from an incomplete, possibly mid-sentence copy of the rules with no signal that anything was missing. Because the manual is no longer committed to a project at all — it is printed on demand by `yg prime`, straight from the installed CLI — there is no file left for any platform to truncate, and the digest committed to the repository is a short, fixed size regardless of how long the full manual is.
- **The local architecture web view (`yg portal`) now attributes a suppression to a component that owns the file through a wildcard or folder mapping.** Its per-component waiver list matched files literally, so a waiver on a file a component owns via `src/**` or a directory mapping was dropped from that component's list even though it appeared in the overall suppression inventory — the view disagreed with itself. Ownership now uses the same wildcard-and-folder-aware matching the scan side uses, so the two agree. (Read-only view only.)
- **`yg owner`, `yg context --file`, and `yg impact --file` no longer name the wrong component when a nested component is configured to cover a broader set of files than its parent.** These three answered "which component owns this file?" with a home-grown rule that picked the longer-matching mapping, while the rest of the tool — including what the check actually verifies — puts hierarchy first (a nested component owns a file it claims inside a parent, regardless of how the two patterns compare in length). The two agree in the ordinary case, but disagreed when a nested component mapped a broader pattern than its parent: the commands reported the parent while the file was verified under the child. All three now defer to the single shared ownership resolver, so they always agree with the component whose rules actually apply.
- **The dependency check no longer passes green over code it could not parse.** If a language's grammar failed to load (a missing or corrupt parser, for example a broken install or a language added without shipping its grammar), the built-in dependency check silently treated every file in that language as having no dependencies — so the whole check could report success over code it never actually analyzed, for an entire language at once. A parse/grammar-load failure now fails the check with a clear, blocking error naming the language, instead of disappearing. A new startup-style test also loads every bundled grammar so a shipping gap is caught directly.
- **Commands no longer crash or fabricate output when a name collides with a built-in object property.** `yg knowledge read`, `yg schemas read`, and `yg impact --type` looked names up in a plain object, so a reserved name like `constructor`, `toString`, or `__proto__` slipped past the "unknown name" guard and either crashed with a generic internal-error message or (for `--type`) printed a fabricated empty report and exited successfully. All three now check for a real, own entry first and give the guided "unknown ..." message with a non-zero exit.
- **"Child wins" ownership is now applied consistently across the remaining checks.** Two more built-in checks — the strict-type coverage scan and the type/`when` classification check — resolved which component owns a file by the first component to match in load order instead of the child-wins rule the rest of the tool uses. A file a more specific child component legitimately claims inside a parent's directory could therefore be checked against the parent's type (a false block) or have its coverage misattributed (a silent miss). Both now use the same shared owner resolution, so ownership agrees everywhere.
- **The suggested "next step" now matches what the report highlights.** In several places `yg check` picked the issue to point you at by internal processing order rather than by priority, so the `Next:` line could name a different rule than the one the grouped/`--top` view shows first, and a warnings-only run whose warning was not a rule violation (a rule past its review date, a high-fan-out or orphaned-rule warning) printed no next step at all. The next-step selection now follows the same priority and ordering as the display in every case.
- **A component that merely depends on a dependency cycle is no longer reported as cyclic itself.** The circular-dependency check flagged nodes that reach a cycle but are not part of one; only the genuine cycle is now reported.
- **Approval runs are safer with the lock and log history.** Several integrity gaps in the fill/approve path are closed: a YAML typo no longer causes an approval to silently delete recorded results for unrelated, unchanged code (pruning waits until the graph loads cleanly); a result for a file that is momentarily unreadable during a run is no longer discarded; the closure step now validates a component's log-history baseline against tampering (a merge or hand-edit) before recording it; and the review-overdue clock is injected rather than read from the ambient system, so a run is reproducible.
- **A tracked file whose name matches a directory-only ignore rule is no longer dropped from a component's verified files.** A `.gitignore` rule like `build/` (directory only) was matched against files as well, so a real file named `build` under such a rule was excluded from a component's hashed file set — a silent gap. The scan now applies the directory-only form only to directories.
- **The local architecture map (`yg portal`) reports several states more honestly.** Flow-level and component-level "checked" now use the same real-verdict signal (a flow no longer reads green off a pairless participant); the boundary view no longer over- or under-reports; and a couple of roll-up/aspect-row derivations were corrected. These affect the read-only view only.
- **`yg drill` reports an unsupported capability instead of a check failure.** A deterministic check that reads a unit's subject files or the JSON/TOML/AST parsers under `yg drill` was misreported as a failing check (exit 2) rather than an unsupported-capability gap (exit 0) — the drill's graph-context guard only covered some of the accessors it should. It now covers the full set, matching the documented contract.
- **Parsers and stores fail more cleanly on malformed input.** A malformed `implies:` in a rule file now yields a structured "invalid" result instead of an uncaught throw that aborts the whole graph load; a project config that is a top-level list (rather than a mapping) is rejected with a clear message; an unreadable schema-version field fails closed; a hand-edited history file missing its final newline appends cleanly; and a log or config with a stray conflict marker is caught. `yg impact --file` / `yg owner --file` given a path outside the project now explain the problem instead of a generic internal-error message.

- **The dependency check no longer blames a parent component for a dependency that belongs to a more specific child that already declared it.** When a component maps a whole directory and a more specific child component claims one file inside it, that file belongs to the child — and so do its outgoing dependencies. The built-in dependency check was still attributing the file's dependencies to the parent (the first component to match it), so a dependency the child had correctly declared showed up as an *undeclared* dependency on the parent, blocking the check with no valid fix — the guidance even pointed at the wrong component's file. The check now agrees with "who owns this file" everywhere else in the tool: the child owns its claimed file, and its dependencies are measured against the child's declared relations. A genuinely undeclared dependency is still caught.
- **A refused check can no longer, in a rare buffering case, exit with a success code.** The step that flushes a large report before the process exits waited on a signal the system only sends after write back-pressure; a small buffered write never triggers it, so the wait could hang — and because the exit code was set *after* that wait, a run that should have failed could fall back to a success exit and skip its own force-exit safeguard. The exit code and the safeguard are now committed before the flush wait, so a refusal always exits non-zero and the report still flushes. (Common runs, which don't hit that buffering state, were unaffected.)
- **Bringing a rule file inside the graph directory under a component no longer blocks the check with a false "excluded by .gitignore" error.** Yggdrasil lets the graph verify its own rule files by mapping them under a component — the graph checking its own rules like any other code. The check that guards against mapping a genuinely ignored file built its "tracked files" list from a repository scan that deliberately skips the graph's own directory (it holds the graph and its rebuildable state, not reviewable source). A committed rule file living there was therefore absent from that list and was wrongly reported as excluded by `.gitignore`, so naming such a file by its exact path failed the check — the very mapping the guidance tells authors to write. Worse, it was inconsistent: writing the same target as a wildcard or a folder slipped through only as a side effect. The check now recognizes that a path inside the graph directory (or under `.git`) is skipped for structural reasons, not because it is ignored, and exempts it — while a source file that really is `.gitignore`d is still refused exactly as before.
- **`yg context --file` now routes its answers to standard output and compares paths consistently.** Two long-standing routing slips are corrected: the "this path is excluded from graph coverage by design" answer (a success, exit 0) and the resolved `file -> node` line were written to the error stream; both are results, so they now go to standard output, leaving the error stream for genuine errors. Separately, the sibling-node suggestion for an unmapped file compared a normalized directory against a raw, un-normalized one, so on Windows-style paths the suggestion could silently come up empty; both sides are now normalized the same way.
- **"Child wins" now works the way it reads: a child node can claim one specific file inside a directory its parent globs, and the child owns it — implicitly, with no error and no restructuring.** Two problems are fixed together. First, three separate places decided which node owns a file and disagreed when two mappings tied, so the built-in dependency check could name a different owner than the rest of the tool and demand you declare (or wrongly reject) a dependency with no valid remedy. All three now share one rule and honour child precedence: a hierarchical child node always wins over its parent, and only among unrelated (sibling) nodes does the more specific mapping break the tie. Second — the substantive fix — the ownership carve-out is now glob-aware: a file a descendant node maps is removed from an ancestor's set even when the ancestor matched it through a glob (previously the carve only fired when the child's mapping string was literally nested under the parent's, so a globbing parent kept co-owning the child's file). The result: `yg check`, `yg owner`, and `yg context` all agree that the child owns its claimed file, the parent's glob owns the rest, and the "special case in a globbed directory" pattern is a first-class, error-free arrangement. Only a genuine conflict between two unrelated (non-hierarchical) nodes claiming the same file is still flagged.
- **A rule that only inspects a component's TYPE or its declared entry points no longer stays "green" after that type or those entry points change.** A checkable rule that read a component's type or ports did not fold those into what its result depends on, so a later change to the type or ports left the old result standing even though the rule might now decide differently. Reading a component's type or ports now records that dependency (exactly as looking the component up in the graph already did), so such a change re-runs the rule at no cost; reading only the component's name, mapping, or files is unaffected. The authoring guide's composition example now carries this note.
- **Corrected a misleading claim in the content-predicate matcher.** The helper that runs a file-content regular expression capped the input length with a comment implying that protected against a runaway (catastrophically backtracking) pattern; it does not — a pathological pattern is exponential in input length and can still stall a synchronous match that cannot be interrupted. Because these patterns are authored inside the human-confirmed configuration, this is a keep-your-patterns-simple footgun rather than an outside attack surface; the comment now says so plainly, and a genuine time limit is noted as a deliberate future change rather than a heuristic that might wrongly reject a valid pattern.

- **A reviewer subprocess that ignores the stop signal can no longer hang a run forever.** When a command-line reviewer overruns its time limit it is asked to stop; a reviewer binary that ignored that request left the run waiting with no bound. It is now force-terminated a short grace period after the initial stop request, so a verification run always makes progress.
- **The hosted-reviewer providers now surface the HTTP status when a request fails, instead of parsing an error response as if it were a verdict.** The Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google reviewer paths did not check whether the request succeeded before reading the reply, so a failed request lost the one diagnostic (the status code) that explains why — matching the check the local providers already perform. The failing status is now recorded (never the response body) and the pair fails closed as before.
- **The Google reviewer and its model-listing / connection-test helpers no longer put the API key in the request URL.** A key in the URL query string leaks into proxy, CDN, and server access logs and any error report that echoes the URL; all three paths now send the key in the request header, matching what the main reviewer path already did.
- **The scan that respects your `.gitignore` now prunes directories ignored by a directory-only pattern.** A pattern ending in a slash (e.g. `build/`) was not matched against candidate directories during the repository scan, so files under such a directory could surface as uncovered; the scan now recognizes the directory form, matching the already-correct behaviour used elsewhere.
- **The project-config rewrite performed during a schema migration is now crash-safe.** The version bump written during an upgrade overwrote the config in place; it now writes through a temporary file and atomic rename, so an interrupt mid-migration can never leave a truncated config on disk — the durability every other committed-state write already has.

- **`yg incident read` now renders every cause tag as inert data, so a hand-edited ledger cannot smuggle control characters onto the terminal.** The incident ledger is a committed file a person edits by hand; the listing echoed each entry's cause tag exactly as written, so a raw control character placed in a tag reached the terminal unchanged. The listing now passes each tag through the same neutralization the attention view already applies to repository-derived text — control characters folded to spaces, surrounding whitespace collapsed — so what prints is always plain, legible text. Recording an incident is unchanged: a new entry's cause is still validated against the fixed set of sanctioned causes.
- **The dependency-structure view's change-reach caption no longer rounds a tiny nonzero reach down to "0%".** The structure view summarizes how far an average change tends to travel as a percentage; a small but nonzero average (well under one percent) rounded to "0%", which reads as "a change reaches nothing" when it genuinely reaches something. A nonzero average now renders as at least "<1%", matching the floor the module loop-share phrase already uses; only an average of exactly zero still reads "0%".
- **The `yg advise` incident counters now read grammatically at exactly one.** The attention view's always-shown incident counter and its wrong-rule evidence line dropped a count into a fixed plural sentence, so a single recorded item read "1 incidents" / "1 wrong-rule incidents". Each line now agrees with its count — "1 incident", "1 wrong-rule incident" — with no other change to when the lines appear or what they report.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** The missing-files rule that guards optional graph and state readers is now reviewed one source file at a time. The component it covers on the store layer maps fourteen files into a single reviewer context near the prompt-size ceiling, so a future edit risked tripping the blocking prompt-too-large gate; the rule is file-local — each optional-file reader stands on its own — so reviewing per file removes the ceiling risk with no change to what the rule enforces.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tests.)** A new parity test binds the portal's dependency-structure panel to the `yg structure` command. The two surfaces each carry their own copy of the graph-relation feeders; the test drives both from one real fixture graph with one shared detected-edge set and asserts their tunnels, module groups, and change reach agree, so a future drift in either copy fails the build.
- **Trying a rule against a specific component no longer falsely warns that the rule is "not attached" when the rule is inherited onto an organizational grouping.** When you exercise a rule against one component, the diagnostic first tells you whether that rule is genuinely in force there — and if not, it notes it is running the rule only as a one-off. That decision now follows the full way a rule reaches a component (including inheritance down the hierarchy) rather than asking whether a result slot happens to exist at that exact component. An organizational grouping that holds no source of its own can still put a rule in force across everything beneath it, with the results landing on the descendants that hold the files; the diagnostic previously misread such a grouping as unattached and printed a misleading one-off notice. The notice is now truthful in both directions, and a not-yet-active rule attached to its own component still correctly reads as attached.
- **`yg advise` no longer crashes on a corrupted local diagnostic record, and every string it shows is inert data.** The attention view reads a local, machine-written log of past rule exercises to flag a regression a rule has stopped catching; a partially written entry in that log — valid JSON on its face but missing the case label the view needs — could make the view abort instead of render. Such an entry is now skipped, exactly as every other malformed entry already is, so the read-only view always renders and exits cleanly. Separately, the few strings the view still printed without quoting — machine-written timestamps in its rationale, and the date echoed back when you defer an item — now pass through the same neutralization as every other displayed value, so no character carried in any shown string can be read as an instruction. Display-only: what the view flags and how a dismiss or defer resolves are unchanged.
- **`yg advise` now renders every piece of repository text as inert data on all of its surfaces, including item identifiers and the built-in health messages.** The attention view already showed a suggestion's evidence (a waiver's own words, a case name, a file and line) quoted as data so it can never be read as an instruction; that same neutralization now also covers each item's stable identifier — printed with `--ids`, listed back when a `dismiss`/`defer` names an item that is not live, and echoed on a successful `dismiss`/`defer` — and the wording of the built-in structural-health suggestions (a rule attached where it matches nothing, a rule referenced by nothing, a rule past its review date). A control character, escape sequence, or line break carried in a repository-derived name can therefore no longer reach an agent reading the view and be mistaken for an instruction. The cleanup is display-only: an item's underlying identity is unchanged, so dismissing or deferring it still resolves exactly as before.
- **The `yg advise` attention line now reports the number of distant dependencies you can actually see, matching `yg structure`.** The line reads "N dependencies jump across distant parts of the architecture — run yg structure to see them", but N counted the entire set of cross-tree dependencies while `yg structure` lists only the farthest few. N is now exactly what `yg structure` displays, so the pointer is literally accurate; both surfaces take that count from one shared definition, so they cannot drift apart again.
- **`yg advise` and `yg aspects --health` no longer raise dead regression-drill alarms for cases that are no longer in the repository's test corpus.** The attention view flags a regression a rule has stopped catching, and the health view cross-references the same record; both read a local, machine-written log of past rule exercises that only ever grows. An exercise recorded against a case since removed or renamed — or measured against a corpus kept outside this repository — lingered in that log and was shown as a live "regression no longer caught" alarm (or read as a rule that "may be weakening"), even though nothing in the repository could re-run or retire such a case; a rule whose whole corpus had been deleted could still show a standing alarm. Both views now consult a recorded exercise only when it came from an in-repository run AND its case still exists in the rule's current test corpus; every orphaned record is ignored (there is nothing left to re-run or retire). Genuine in-corpus regressions are unaffected, the corpus lookup is fail-open so both views stay read-only and always succeed, and the local log itself is untouched.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tests.)** A test that stands up a throwaway git repository as a fixture can no longer touch this repository's real git index. The test suite spawns many `git init` / `add` / `commit` fixtures; under full-suite parallelism one such fixture operation could reach the real repository (through an inherited git environment variable or by walking up from a working directory inside the repo) and reset its index — and when the suite runs inside the pre-commit gate, that reset could land mid-commit, so a gate reporting success could still capture only part of the staged change set (a "green build that lies"). Every fixture git command is now pinned to its own fixture directory with an explicit git directory, which disables all repository discovery, backed by a worker-level boundary that scrubs inherited git variables and caps upward repository discovery at the repo root. A new regression guard fails if a future test can once again mutate the real index. Test isolation only — no assertion or CLI behavior changed.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tests.)** The spawn-smoke test that exercises the offline escape-scan script now carries its own explicit 30-second budget, so it no longer flakes under full-suite parallelism. That test spawns a Node subprocess which walks the repository's whole git history; when the entire suite contends for the machine at once, that walk can run long, and without a timeout of its own the test could trip the default per-test deadline on a loaded runner and fail intermittently — a flake with nothing to do with the code under test. The explicit budget removes that contention-induced boundary. Test-timing only — no assertion, script, or CLI behavior changed.
- **Trying a checkable rule against an example file that can't be fully parsed now behaves exactly like the enforcing gate, instead of refusing to evaluate it.** When you exercise a rule against example files (with `yg drill` or `yg aspect-test`), an example whose source could not be cleanly parsed — a genuine syntax error, or a raw control byte that corrupts the file — was aborted as "could not evaluate", even though the very same file is judged normally during `yg check`. A rule that inspects a file's raw text rather than its parsed structure (for example, one that scans for raw control bytes) could therefore never be exercised against such an example. The exercise commands now hand the check the same best-effort parse the enforcing run does, so a text-based rule runs identically in both, and a rule that reads the parsed structure receives the same partial parse it would at the gate. Among other things this lets a raw-control-byte example be kept as an ordinary same-type example file rather than a workaround in a different file type.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** The rule that forbids logging raw provider data no longer rejects the natural inline redaction form. The deterministic check that guards the LLM files against writing raw `prompt` / `response` / `content` / `body` to a debug log used to pass only the assign-then-log shape (`const safe = redactSecrets(prompt); debugWrite(safe)`) and wrongly refused the equivalent inline `debugWrite(redactSecrets(prompt))`, because it only looked for a redaction wrapper among the log call's ancestors, never inside the logged argument. It now recognizes a `redactSecrets(...)` wrapper around the logged value itself. The recognition is per-argument and per-occurrence, so a raw value sitting beside — or concatenated or interpolated with — a redacted one (`debugWrite(redactSecrets(prompt), response)`, `debugWrite(redactSecrets(prompt) + response)`, `` debugWrite(`${redactSecrets(prompt)} ${response}`) ``) is still refused and no genuine leak is silenced; new pass/fail examples pin both the accepted inline form and each of those non-masking guards.
- **`yg aspects --health` now labels a content-less bundle rule by its true kind.** The health table's kind column read each rule's declared reviewer field directly, so a bundle rule that only groups other rules — shipping no reviewer definition of its own — could show as a reviewer-backed (LLM) rule when its definition mistakenly named one. The column now derives the kind from what the rule actually ships: a written rule reads as reviewer-judged, a local script as deterministic, and a definition that only bundles other rules as an aggregate — so a bundle can never be mistaken for a rule that runs a reviewer.
- **`yg structure` no longer rounds a real cycle down to "0%".** In the Modules section, a small but nonzero share of cross-group dependencies that form a cycle (for example a fraction well under one percent) rounded to "0% … are part of a cycle", which reads as "no cycles" when cycles genuinely exist. A nonzero share now renders as at least "<1%"; only when the share is exactly zero does the line state that every dependency flows one way.
- **`yg owner` without a file now explains what to do instead of printing a bare argument error.** Running `yg owner` with no `--file` printed the argument parser's terse "required option not specified" line. It now emits the standard what/why/next message — what is missing, why the command needs it, and the exact form to re-run — and still exits non-zero.
- **A deliberate whole-file waiver placed at the top of a file is now reported consistently wherever the inventory appears.** The recent change that recognizes a top-of-file bare disable as an intentional whole-file exemption (`file-level`, no warning) reached the `yg suppressions` inventory but not the machine-readable suppression inventory the dashboard data feed builds from — so the very same marker was labeled a sanctioned whole-file waiver in one place and an "unbounded range" risk in the other. Both surfaces now derive that classification from the same signal, so they can never disagree on the same marker; a genuine mid-file unclosed waiver is still flagged in both. What each reviewer actually waives is unchanged — this is a reporting-consistency fix only.
- **The per-rule health table's wildcard-waiver note now reads grammatically when exactly one wildcard waiver exists.** `yg aspects --health` ends with a note summarizing wildcard waivers (which silence every rule and are therefore counted separately); with a single such waiver it read "1 wildcard suppress marker apply … and is not counted", leaving the verb in the plural. The verb is now conjugated to the count — "1 … marker applies", "2 … markers apply".
- **(Repo-internal dogfood tooling.)** `scripts/lock-history-audit.mjs` no longer contains embedded NUL bytes (a map-key separator was written as a raw NUL rather than the `\0` escape), which had made git classify the whole script as binary — un-diffable and un-blameable. The separator is now written as the escape, so the source is plain text; the runtime key is byte-identical, so the audit's laundering-signature detection is unchanged.
- **The dogfood showcase's `when:`-filter count and graph-validation flow description now match the actual graph.** The showcase stated that six aspects carry a `when:` applicability filter — there are ten — and that the graph-validation flow applies two rules to three components, when it applies three (including the missing-files guard) to four. Both figures are corrected to the real numbers so the case study describes the graph as it actually stands.
- **The deterministic-aspect cookbook's quick-reference table no longer models the traversal bug it warns about.** `yg knowledge read writing-deterministic-aspects` warns that a `walk` visitor must `return` (not `return false`) on a non-matching node, or the walk stops at the root and inspects nothing — but its own quick-reference table still showed `walk(rootNode, n => n.type === 'call_expression')` one-liners, whose arrow visitor returns a boolean `false` for every non-matching node: exactly that bug. Those rows are now written as node-type matches to test inside a visitor, so an adopter copying the table can no longer reproduce the vacuous-check trap; the worked example and the function-boundary row (the one intended full `walk(...)` call) are unchanged.
- **The "redact provider data before logging it" rule now actually guards the LLM call chain — previously it matched nothing and enforced nowhere.** The rule that forbids writing raw prompt or response text to any log, file, or console before redaction was wired through an applicability path that reached no code, so it looked enforced but silently checked nothing. It is now attached directly to the code that builds reviewer prompts, dispatches them to the LLM provider, and runs LLM diagnostics, and it is reviewed one source file at a time so it applies uniformly to every file in that code without overflowing a reviewer's context. Every one of those files is now checked against the rule.
- **The exit-codes rule description no longer forbids what its check and the code legitimately do.** The rule's description claimed only a single failure exit code was allowed, but its own check — and the CLI's actual behavior — permit both a clean exit and a failure exit (a few commands must exit explicitly with success after finishing their work). The description now matches the check: exit codes 0 and 1 are allowed, and only exit code 2 or higher is forbidden.
- **(Repo-internal dogfood graph.)** Two deterministic rules nested three directories deep — the relations-reference catalogue-versus-matrix-test correspondence checks — were skipped when every deterministic rule was first labeled with its error direction, because that sweep only looked two directory levels deep. Both now carry a label: the catalogue-has-a-test check is `over` (its forward arm flags a case as untested whenever a plainly-written `it('<id>')` is absent, so a real test written in a focused, skipped, or template-literal form is falsely flagged), and the case-is-really-exercised check is `under` (it fires only on a found test block proven defective and stays silent on absent or dynamically-shaped blocks). The label is pure metadata, never part of any stored verdict, so adding it re-verified nothing; the aspect-drills census table is updated to match.
- **The CLI-command-contract rule's write-up of the bootstrap `init` exception no longer describes behavior the code does not have.** The rule text claimed `init` reaches for the graph loader directly on its rules-refresh path and is covered by a waiver — but `init` does neither. As the one command that must run before a graph exists, it checks for the graph directory itself through a shared guard that emits the standard missing-graph message, calls no graph loader, and needs no waiver; the text is corrected to describe that real bootstrap path. The pair is green today, but any future edit to `init` would have re-run the reviewer against the false exception and risked a spurious refusal — the fix forecloses that.
- **The universal installer's empty-directory cleanup no longer depends on the host OS's native path separator.** The helper that removes a now-empty parent directory left behind by a swept legacy artifact tested containment by string-prefixing the platform's own separator onto the root path — correct on every platform on its own, but inconsistent with the rest of the codebase's separator-agnostic path handling. It now tests containment with a relative-path comparison that works identically regardless of which separator the underlying paths use. Behavior is unchanged; this closes a portability inconsistency before it could compound with the string-prefix approach elsewhere.
- **A failed read of a committed agent-rules file no longer disappears without a trace.** The boundary that reads `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, and `.clinerules/yggdrasil.md` for the digest-staleness gate treated a read failure the same as a normal absence (returning "no file"), with no diagnostic recorded either way. A read that fails for a reason other than the file simply not existing — a permissions problem, for instance — now writes a debug-log line naming the file and the underlying error, so the cause is visible when debug logging is enabled instead of being silently indistinguishable from "the file was never installed." A file that is simply absent records nothing: that is the ordinary state of an optional artifact (a project with no `.clinerules/` directory, or one where the install was never run), the check already reports it as a missing artifact, and a diagnostic line there would be noise on the most common path.
